The Duchcsa of Saxe-Coburg-Gotha, who on Monday' this week celebrated the anniversary of her birthday, is a very handsome woman, and looks considerably younger than her years, which now number forty-five.l She has been a grandmother a little more than five years Prince Carol of Roumania having been born on October 15th 1893. The Duchess is undoubtedly the most statelyl of the Queen's daughters-in-law, and as aunt of the present Czar, and wife of a reigning "niece, a certain ' touch of hauteur in her manner is perhaps only suitable to her position. She dresses exceptionally well, wed has taught her three married daughters to follow closely in her footsteps. She is devoted to gardenias and is never seen without them. • Lady Algernon, ho is one of the brightest and most fascinating women in society,' lacks the perfect regue larity of feature that distinguishes her sister the Countess of Warwick, but her face has equal charm, owing to its sweet expression, the brilliant blue of her eyes, and her exquisite complexion. Jet is in great request at the present time, owing to so many clistingsrehed families being plunged into ' mourning. The most exquisite and fanciful deceive are being produced, and public taste in this, as in other things, is yearly improving. Jet is a variety of coaly matter, resembling cannel coil, but harder and blacker, It also takes a higher polish. It is, like ordinary coal, a eombination of carbon and hydrogen. Its original source is doubtful, but it appears rather to have been bitumen then woody matter, although woody structure and scales of fish etc., are sometimes traceable, It has evidently been long used for ornamental purposes. for In a Roman tumulus at Whitby, a jet earring was found, with a female skeleton, and at the church of St. Gereion, Cologne, a complete set of jet ornaments were found in a stone ceffin. The miner starts wee king with the greatest care, hunt" leg through rocks until he fiads the upper bias form itinn which produces the best jet, the under quelity which I. easier to work and finer and helter than the soft, which is proportionately duller and more brittle. It is found in layers varying in thickness an average being teree to eighteen feet broad, and ten to twelve feat beg. The Spaniards at one time exported large quantities of jet Into this country, but was found to be in no way so dur- able as home produce. A HINT TO SIIFFEHERS.—A neighbour's chickens annoyed a lady in a Gloucestershire village by rooting up her garden. Tun LIMIT.—A man had a big trade in Albata spoons, and he insisted that he must have a better price. The manufacturer said he could not make any reduction unless he put in more lead. The merchant said: "All right; use more lead." It was not long before he wanted another reduction, and he got the same answer from the manufacturer. He get instructions from the merchant to use more lead, as he must have the spoons at a less price. Still he was not happy, and wanted another reduc- tion. The manufacturer informed him that it was impossible; that he could not make any lower price. The merchant wrote back, and said: "Put in more lead." The manufacturer answered, and said • "I can't do it, as the last lot I sent you were all lead." Causic FOB. Mr. Hombeak "Mercy, Ezra I How could anybody be happy in such a place as that?" LADTES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE, Something approaching a revolution has taken place in the shape of hats thisiptumn. No longer are their brims turned up against the crown at the back or over the left ear, but they droop meekly over forehead and coil of hair, and are only very slightly raised at the sides by means of trimmings. This particular shape reminiscent of the fashions of the sixties, is iikely to be very much worn— indeed, setting aside the always-popular boat arid and sailor shapes, it has no dangerous rivals except the toque and the tricorn hat, and as it suits the majority of English faces it seems likely to remain iu favour throughout the coming winter.

One of the prettiest of these drooping-brimmed hats is of gray soft felt, trimmed with. three huge soft ostrich plumes starting from a big rosette of shaded grey velvet in front, falling towards the sides and partly sailing the crown, which is low, as are all hat-crowns this season. The feathers are grey, like the hat itself but a pleasant touch of colour is provided by the velvet chrysanthemums of rich orange which are tucked under the brim at either side. This brim, by the way, is quite as broad at the back as it is in front, unlike a few of the new models whose brims are eloped away slightly behind.* Coloured felts, with contrasting linings— especially in deep green combined with turquoise blue—are again very fashionable, and a smart hat of this sort has its crown encircled by two feathers shaded from green to blue and starting from a mighty rosette of miroir velvet that has a hand- some paste ornament in the centre. A grey felt hat, lined with crimson and trimmed with grey feathers, shaded crimson wings, and steel orna- ments, is also a bright and pretty headgear for wear on a iull autumnal day.

In addition to big cloaks we have to weleome back that most obliging of garments, the redin- gote. Girls aver that very large coats are not only heavy but weight their skirts down to such an extent that they spoil anything smart and new. My advice to them is not to wear a good skirt underneath a smart new coat. This is one reason that a coat is such a useful garment. It is worn both for cold weather and for driving and travelling in, and with it may go a skirt that has known its best days and is not longer than the redingote it- self. If the skirt is longer and shows at all lb ii apt to look shabby. It is modish to line a coat of this sort with a delicate shot glace silk. Anemone oolour is a particular favourite; cyclamen pink another. I have Been a green very successfully lining a putty-coloured cloth. Glace silk comes as a boon and a blessing in this direction; it slips on and off so easily and seems to wear a very great deal better than the striped satins which are cer- tainly thicker than this silk, but not nearly so com- fortable nor so durable.

üb.tri

For a young girl an artistic little dram am be made of electric blue cloth or velveteen with a pleated vest, aed a soft sash of silk in a paler shade of blue. The gouged sleeves are also of silk. With this is worn a large picture hat which is made of velveteen to match the dress, with a puff- ing at the edge and a cluster of ostrich tips in the front. A dress, becoming only to young girls and very young women, is one which fastens invisibly under the arm. Even these bring it nearer to a bodice with embroidery, simulating a short bolero, or with insertions of braid arranged in clusters, encircling the skirt, and repeating themselves on the shoulders. A quadruple collar, trimmed with similar insertion, completes this costume, which is destined for walking, travelling, races, or country excursions. This implies that the high, volumin- ous collar, far from being abandoned, reappears with the autumn days, indispensable on a carriage cloak, a theatre cloak, a cloak for every occasion ! When in slate-blue on brick-coloured cloth, it is bordered with transparent incrustations on a silk lining. Two or three exquisite! have appeared at country houses with collars, painted by themselves on cloth, in lovely f? ‘levicee. The effect is most elegant and rech, - and there is little fear of the mode becoming t, .n. "'stir loving_ al RIMEL

c THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, OCTOBER 21 1898

,(11 rights reserved.) THE

SECRET OF MARK PEPYS.

BY FRED J. PROCTOR, Author of _"Timothy Twill's Secret," "Jean

Marlou." ‘Dr. Dobbs." (tc.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: This story is related by Sandy Does, a Californian trader before the days of the gold rush. His employer, Mark Pepys, is in love with a Mexican girl, Lucia Diaz. Manuel Muro, a Mexican also appears to love the girl, and Dees fears that he is plotting to take Pepys's life. He doubts the girl's good faith also. One night Pepys's store is ransacked and a half- breed on guard is killed. There is reason to believe that Muro and a band of Indians have had a hand in the affair. Pepys takes an oath to revenge the half• - breed. Sandy Dees discovers that Pepys has a secret which prevents him ever returning to his native land, and the Scot tries to discover its nature. The settlement of San Leno, beseiged by Indians, sends for help. Mark and his friends, with Lucia, proceed thither.

===== 0002601_18981021_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER VIL

TIIE SIEGE,

The lion in Mark Pepys was roustedtind the redskins would feel his teeth. As the Indians had removed their women and children to the recesses of the forest, ive anticipated a vigorous attack. Our gunners were rather short of powder, so we reserved the -howitzers for masses of -the enemy, and prepared for a sortie. Only those who have been within the gates of a beleagured city, fort, or camp can realise the anxious hearts which beat at San Leno as Mark led his brave men into the teeth of the enemy. Only a few Indians-could be seen hovering on' the ontskirtS of the • planta- tions, but we soon challenged proof whether others were in hiding. As a preliminary, manceuvre, Mark demolished the le'vers and removed the buckets from the wells used by the red converts. Next he levelled a brick-kiln, and then prudently approached the nearest ;huts. Fortunately, there was no need for extreme,caution, for the Indian adobes were deserted ;and as the hatchets, hanimers, and the fire-fiend did their 'work any redskins who lurked about peered at us on horseback from a safe distance, or keenly watched our raamfeuvres from the depths of the woods. The cunning enemy had. removed all his valuables, and Mark was em- bittered because, after all his efforts to deal fairly by the Indians, they had stolen every portable article—tools, sacks of grain, leather, raw hides, fruit, and saddles. Fortunately for us the granaries and store-houses within the ramparts were filled. Annihilation might be our lot, but, happily, not starvation.

For fully three hours our men carried on their work unmolested, when from the Presidio tower I saw the northern horizon swarming with foes. The men who were fixing wires to short stakes just around the fort, so as to trip the Indians in the event of a sudden rush, were brought in. "Pecos, while I hoist the red danger-flag, do thou give warning below. The dusky warriors swarm over the hills like ants. Tell the Sefiorita what thou dost behold." _ .

Pecos, nimble as a kitten, flew down the stone steps, and soon I saw a messenger issue forth to warn the pioneers. Theretipon great efforts were made to fire every hut within two hundred yards of the walls; but this feat had not been acccom- plished before the • galloping of a host of braves came unpleasantly near, and Mark's contingent sought a safer retreat within the fort. "Sandy," observed my chief, "you have always said that money is therrhost powerful engine on earth to move men, but I think jealousy is ita twin. I have never truckled to the military governors; they are jealous of my popularity and power, and I believe this organisation to be of their making. Mercenary troops were never very reliable, and it will depend upon the number of Californians who lead and assist the redskins whether they maintain the fight. If I come well out of this, the first opportunity that offers I strike against Mexican rule." "Hush!" said I. "Hauld yer tongue! That ugly Californian eunuch's listening. Ye re dashini an' bold enough fer a soldier, but ye wouldna mak an ambassador. Ye're too ootspoken an' honest." "I don't fear eavesdroppers so much as dusky traitors. While you hoisted the red flag I saw the Indian guide through my glass shoot an arrow at a venture, northward, in the direction of the incoming host. These cunning fellows know how to convey messages by such means. But I'll not condemn where I can't prove guilt. Listen! The foe has halted; he is 'covorting,' as Harry Puke would say." We felt convinced that representations were already made of our presence, and the new methods of defence adopted by us, for presently certain evolutions boded ill. Their riflemen completely encircled us, and a large body worked strenuously to stop the conflagrations. • We refrained from firing, in order to keep our ammunition for a closer encounter, a precaution which afterwards proved of immense advantage, but enabled the invading host to secure several ambuscades. As Mark explained, "It is the case of a chess-player sacrificing the pawns to save the big pieces." Finding our plan was giving the enemy con- fidence, we set our crack shots to pick off such as exposed themselves within range, and this had the effect elf curbing their ardour. But it increased their cunning, for through my glass I saw a con- ference take place between their leaders. Their aides-de-camp were despatched with new instruc- tions, and a general movement of retreat followed, the "braies maintaining their circle, but at a greater distance. "The delay of offensive warfare means that an attack is contemplated under cover of the darkness." This was Mark's opinion. The attack was nothing short of murder—an attack afterwards justified by intelligent Indians on the ground that the Spanish cohorts had devastated Mexico, and robbed the natives of their inheritance.

During a period of suspense rifle holes were knocked in the adobe outhouses, weak spots strengthened, and a barricade, COT isting of sacks of flour, grain, furniture, branches, &c., erected inside the gates. I recall with pride the ringing cheers which greeted Mark's last instructions tc the assembled garrison, and the look of brave determination on our faces when his words of ckeer inspired us with greater confidence in his leadership. • "Comrades," said he, doffing his hat, "the red- skins outside mean business." "Bo do we I " replied the lads. • "They have repaid my kindness with base ingratitude. They have stolen my cattle, and laid waste my lands. Now they wish to exterminate us. If I attempted to conciliate them it would only be but a temporary release from a horrible death. So we must fight to the bitter end. The women, under the instructions of Senorita Diaz, will attend to the wounded, and carry such as cannot walk to the temporary hospital within our hat refuge, the Presidio. No man is to leave hie post, but if hard pressed he can call for help. All the ammunition will be given out; there is not sufficient for a second supply. Take steady aim, that each shot may tell. Let every bullet strike home with deadly effect, for the skilled marksman wins to-day. If we resist the first rush all will end well; but, if from a thirst for blood and a desire to plunder, white men are directing the attack, we may expect them to rally. They will give no quarter; so give none. Remember what it insane if any of us fall into their hands—torture, and a lingering death. I have made a vow not to surrender. Do you agree ? " "Yea, yes!" they cried as one man; and, judging from the resolution upon their faces, they meant it. If there is one dissentient, let him now speak," added Mark; but all were silent. "At the sound of the bugle," he continued with a more confident ring in his voice, his face radiant with a smile of satisfaction, "those who are able will make for the Presidio—our last point of resistance. If there is danger of our women and children falling alive into their hands, let those who remain take counsel how to rob the fiends of their victims. If I fall, the command will devolve on my friend, Sandy Dees,"—he shook me heartily by the hand— "and I ask you to obey him as you would me. In such an event I commend the Senorita to your care. Should Sandy sums-arab, Father Otando will lead you. Good hick must determine the rest. GcC. help us!" The brave lads then raised a mighty shout, and after Father Otando had given us his blessing, and the men had solemnly taken leave of those dear to them, Mark assigned each man his post, with instructions that needed courage alone to make the defenders fight and die as warriors ought. Mark and I ascended the Prisidio tower hb watch the movements of the enemy, and while there Lucia came up rwith a scared countenance. This woman, whose determination equalled her beauty, was a passle to me, so when she asked for ury teiescooe and peered between the buttresses

Senorita Diaz, 1

the foe, I stood back and gazed at her with astonishment. Mark was looking to the condition of his rifles, and putting a keen edge on his hunt-, ing knife, for he expected tough work with these desperate fellows. "-Mark," observed Lucia, as she returned my glass, "if there is found a traitor amongst us what shall be his fate? " "Instant death, Lucia. Don't play with fire; put it out. Who is not actively with me at this crisis is my foe." "Then arm each woman as best you can. I have a stiletto and a brace of pistols. Should anyone amongst us play you false he shall lack the second opportunity. Let Pecos load your rifles; as for the Indian guide, he shall precede me whithersoever, I direct, and do my bidding. Let your most- trusted Englishmen be about you too, for they are not likely to play you false, and an Indian will not Suspect a woman of sufficient'vigilariee or courage to avenge treachery. This is simply a precautionary step, for all may be well with us. Father Otando can manage the Spaniards; I will keep an'erye on this Californians. Are the guns in good condition ? " "First-class," answered Mark. "But why this caution? Does anybody's courage need screwing up ? " She took my glass, and han ing it to Mark, said: "Look for yourself .14 yonder stack of dry brush- wood. Tell me if you see anything strange?" Lucia stood with folded arms, the sentiment subdued within her, .and every movement indica- ting presence of. mind combined with: singular courage. Mark levelled • the, telescope, and I strained my eyes to discover the object whieh gave a flush to tuciii's cheeks, a sparkle of excitement to her dark ,eyes,,. and an expression ofextra- ordinary resolution to her face. "I see nothing uncommon," Mark quietly observed. "You are fanciful, Lucia." " Mas vale ser necio quo porfiado !:?' ,exclsimed she, annoyed. - But my friend Mark was neither foolish nor obstinate, • - The moment I spied out the enemy,' prostrated myself, crying: - "Doon, baith o's ye!" We crouched beneath the. adobe battlements, a a shbwer of arrows struck the tower or flew past us. "A narra squeak!" I muttered, with a shudder. "The fight begins!" cried Lucia, her blood warm and her eyes flashing. "Let. our defence be such as they will never forget. I have learnt something about Indian tactics. First, let your gunners dis- lodge the party behind that stack, for we have not only to face the Indians; but to worst their leaders, who are white men with military experience." -I,direeted my glass upon the stack, and noted one or. two in European dress. "Yes, Lucia's richt. Gie them pepper!" The episodes between Muro and Mark flashed across my mind, and I wondered whether the rival had a hand in this nefarious design upon our persons and, property. The Senorita threw her arms around Mark's neck and kissed him fervently. "My love," she said, "none of us know what the next sunrise may reveal, but be assured of this, I shall not fall alive into the hands of this merciless foe." The significance of this remark was afterwards made apparent. "I swear," observed Mark, "that the black- guards shall not make sport of me " ; and kissing her with a fondness which hardly dove-tailed with the stern work in hand, he added: "You must stay here—Nrlth Sandy, while 1 point the northern howitzers." "Luelc t'yer, aim," said I, as Mark rushed doYhi• the •stairs. And then, when I turned to look at the Senorita,- she was peering with an anxious face in the direction of the stack. I touched her shoulder gently, saying: "Senorita, ,it was your own choice to run into this danger.' • The whole majesty of her nature' asserted itself as she proudly answered: "When I complain it will le time for you to • chide, not befo,re. Men cry out about their little aches and pains, for- getting what women can patiently suffer. Men are moved to heroic deeds by great excitement; it is in the nature of women to need but the call of duty. Be the end what it may, I shall not be charged with cowardice."

I shrugged my shoulders nen-Plussed. We waited with bated breath, while I directed my glass througlian embrasure, Lucia, looking over., ray shoulder to note the effect of the guns. Presently the boom and smoke announced that our resistance had begun in earnest. The first shot ' ploughed the ground near the stack, but the second scattered the wood in all directions, and sent about a dozen horsemen romping to the rear. Lucia took the glass from me with considerable excite- ment, and, directing it upon the retreating foe, exclaimed : "My God! It is Muro I We shall have hot work! "Muro?" I reiterated. • Lucia checked herself, and then, with a winning smile, answered: "I thought I recognised the notorious Muro, but, at this distance, deception is easy. It may be a Spanish cavalier and not the outlaw who, like Satan, is painted blacker thaa he is." "Those who should, dare not betray Muro." Lucia shrugged her shoulders and raised her eyebrows. I looked yet again, but the leaders had sought ambush further afield, and the whole of the horsemen surrounding the fort had not added to their numerical strength, but simply extended their ring. I reported these manoeuvres to Mark, who gave orders that the lads were to fire at those patrols they were pretty sure of hitting. Our desultory firing told home with such deadly effect that, as the gloaming increased, the foe hid him- self behind every available shelter. Our garrison got impatient, and Pecos rendered great service by suggesting that we should occupy their minds and hands in making resin-wood torches and paste fire- balls for casting among the besiegers. This was an effective mode of warfare in the Far West when the Indians were sufficiently strong to attack in force, and before the introduction of :hulleta with such marvellous penetration at long range. A night attack would increase our 'danger and lessen their risk. Indeed, the suspense became so great as the shadows crept over the gardens, that we eagerly looked for an unclouded sky, and, fortunately for us, the stars twinkled with welcome brilliancy, and the moon rose in all her silvery splendour with a light so powerful that our , tallow candles and oil limps were hardly needed„, "The Indians are bound to strike at once," I ' observed to Lucia; "for another day's delay might bring us reinforcements and add" to their dis- comfiture • though I sincerely hope the waggons. from Yerha Buena are sufficiently distant not to cause a brush with the enemy's rear." As the moonlight strengthened we felt our success was assured, but the fate of the approaching party would depend on their readiness in case of sudden surprise. After all these years I vividly recall this scene. I remember taking out my watch precisely at 9.4.5 p.m., as the word was passed round that the front line of Indians was stealthily advancing; and the owl-eyed amongst us could discern the red- skins crawling, creeping, and darting from tree to tree, while the stillness of the beautiful night was only broken by the crisp sounds of our rifles as we replied to the intermittent discharge of their arrows. Stationed as I was upon the tower, I could note the general method of attack and defence. From individuals at other points of the fort I obtained the information necessary to give some details of the fight. At first the few Indians that had rifles dis- charged them in the teeth of the guns, but we reserved our powder and shot for more tangible targets thlin the shadowy pickets. As it turned out this manoeuvre of the enemy was a subterfuge to distract our attentioa; in fact, a wily pretext to obtain a favourable opportunity for a rush upon us while our gunners were reloading. The silence of our guns perplexed them, but in fifteen minutes I shouted down to Mark: "The enemy is gathering in force at the four corners, but especially at the north-east. Let drive at 'em!" Hardly had the words escaped my lips when the latter contingent made a rush for the corner, and before the balls could do much damage, the main body had cleared the north-east angle of fire. This stratagem was repeated at the other corners almost simultaneously. The sharpshooters at the apparent points of attack fired away with spirit, and the guns on the south and west did mucu destruction, owing to the evident blunder of ts., warriors in exposing their solid ranks to the raking fire. The north-east party was better led than the jest, for instead of endeavouring to scale the out-houses or knock down the corner buttresses they made for the ramparts and gates close under cover of the walls. If Muro were there, he was leading them. Finding by these smart tactics that I was com- paratively useless on the tower, I ran down at break-neck speed, and, tearing aeross the court- yard, sprang upon the ramparts by my leader's side. "Sandy," spake Mark fiercely, "this is not the tower."

"Forgie me, Mark," I replied, "I couldna gulp doon the thocht o' ye bein, left filmic wi' these cut-throat sackets! If it. means death, I've brocht mysel' to die game at your side!" My Scotch blood was up, and as ths rust red- skins just then arrived in front of dm rampart, Mark cried: "Stand by, gunners. Arrrel . . . Good 1 " he added, seeing the devastatioa which that discharge caused among their serried ranks. "Re-load, and mow them down!" The deep-sot curses of our lads mingled with the Indians' yells and whoops, and before the smoke had properly cleared we were having a hand-to- hand fight between and around the guns. Hatchet met hatchet, fist struck fist, foe wrestled with foe in deadly encounter, and the Herculean efforts of Mark Pepys can never be effaced from my memory. Those who hold the coveted "Victoria Cross" are not all the bravest in battle. On one occasion he rushed between my prostrate body and the uplifted 4,...ahawk of a stalwart redskin. • Having lost his :lA. and being wounded in his right hand, Mark ~ruck the Indian such a blow on the throat with his left hand that the enemy reeled. My pistol was levelled, and seizing an opportunity when the muscular, fellow was raising his tomahawk for a crushing blow at my friend, I discharged. it mint blank into his heart. He fell with a heavy,a2nd. Thereupon Mark seized his, axe and belaboured the foe, which now wavered a bit and fell back. It was but the retreating wave preparing with renewed strength to beat again upon the shore.

. CHAPTER VIII. THE MILK OF HERBS. The besiegers soon rallied. We, had no time to :sea who fell among our party, but, our pistols did excellent service. When our firearms were dis- charged our leader's stentorian voice was heard above the din; "Charge!" We rushed between the guns, and with fixed bayonets, crowbsrs, axes, and spears fell upon our assailants with fearful effect. I remember springing with such impetuosity at, a dusky fellow that ~when I plunged, my bowie-knife into his heart he clutched me , so firmly in his arms that Mark's hand 'alone saved me falling headlong with him down the ramparts. -Our stout resistance curbed the foe, and those who had escaped our weapons made for the gate, which they were endeavouring to break down with hatchets and rude battering rams. From our vantage ground-we" poured a raking fire • upon them, and judging-by the yells and imprecations there was bloody work in that spot. "The -broken' glass we strewed over the around has Cut their feet," observed Mark. "They will no* attempt to smash in the gate. We must concentrate a lot of strength there. Come 'lads' let'them have a deadly fire! " And now they lively Pecos bethought him of a most effective plan. He lit the fireballs, and threw them among the enemy; and we followed suit by I hinging lighted torchea, resin wood, and oiled rags where they pressed closest. The distinct advantage of this was to frighten and burn the barefooted, and we found that every now and then their leaders were compelled to hound them on to renewed attacks. Such as came near Mark stood a poor chance of escape; he was reckless of danger, and his courage inspired us all with that spirit of light which knows no surrender. Soldiers and sailors. will bear me out, that the stage of anticipa- tion is the worst part of a battle, for men whose nervous, system might be paralysed with craven fear put a spurt on under pressure; and when once in the thick of the fight, it is_then simply a desire to be up and at the enemy! I had no time to wonder how Lucia and the other defenders were faring, so hot and continuous was the contest in our quarter; and as the old gates were giving way our danger was imminent. Just Alien a messenger came across and shouted, into Mark's ear, "Senorita Diaz sends for you. The cursed Indians hey' made a breach in the southern wall!" Mark turned to me, "Sandy, spare me a couple of lads. Keep the Indians at bay from behind the barricade, but watch the ramparts. If I sound a retreat, spike the guns!" • With that he rushed to Lucia's assistance; and I tell you it was a warm tussle with us at the gates for the next twenty minutes. At that spot our powder was exhausted, and on the ramparts it was getting low. Most of the foes' arrows were Used, so now the fight was with tomahawks, clubs, knives, and cutlasses. Were my mortal course to run a century, I could never forget how our lads fought for life during that half hour. When the gates gave way and the "braves" leaped over the prostrate forms of their comrades, their howls of disappointment on seeing the barrier were fiendish. We answered with shouts of exultation, which for a moment staggered them. We were not idle during those five minutes, and many a redskin bit the dust before he could scramble up the barrier. As for me, I was determined to die on the spot rather than budge an inch. "Gie 't 'em hot, my lads!" I shouted, as the Indians scrambled upon the barrier like locusts, those behind filling up the gaps. "It's a fight fer life noo I At 'em!" Those on the ramparts on each side were pressed bard, but each man maintained his ground until struck down or carried to the Presidio by the small ambulance corps. "Here, pard," I said hastily to a wiry Cali- fornian who was fleet of foot, "Gang awa to every rampart. When the bugle sounds Ist 'ern spike the guns an' rush fer the Presidio." And, turning to a second man, I cried: "Find the captain, sta' tell him it'll be impossible fer us to last muckle langer wi'oot ilka ane bein' cut doon I Ask him what we're to do?" Parry and blow, yells and curses rent the air, and the glorious moon aided our defence. We were driven to the last extremity when the clarion note sounded above the awful din, and then cover. ing our retreat as best we could the whole of the survivors sought the last chance of self-preservation. "Guid God!" I muttered, driven to desperation, "Why hast Thou forsaken us? " But my words were beaten back upon me by the din and con- fusion of the onslaught. • On reaching the Presidio, the fearful struggle that had taken place on the southern rampart was only too apparent by the number of wounded and dying. Father Otando and the women were busy, but Lucia Diaz had her left arm bound with a bloodstained handkerchief, a bullet having inflicted an ugly flesh wound. It turned out that when Mark rushed to her assistance the Indians were already through the wall; and notwithstanding the marvellous efforts of our leader, superior numbers compelled him to fall behind a granary, and at '.:ast seek refuge in the Presidio. The number of Indian bodies stretched inside the breach testified to the stout resistance made by Mark and his com- panions at that point. All round the ramparts not a man had shirked his duty, and by the pale and haggard faces of those gathered within the Presidio I saw the desperate • straits to which we were all driven. , Lucia now supplied us with barley water—next to , bear's fat, perhaps the most sustaining nourish- ment for great physical strain. "Defend the alcoves," commanded Mark, sand 11 a man to every loophole within the building!" "Shall we not cut through the enemy and each take his chance ? " asked one, for a moment for- getting the weaker sex. "No!" thundered Mark in reply; "it would be cowardly and base to leave the wounded and the women and children in the hands of such merciless brutes. Let the wounded who are able load the rifles. If we can hold out till daylight, there will be a chance of measuring the strength of the enemy. Don't waste your ammunition —every cartridge may be worth a life." Mark was a veritable Samson against the Philis- tines. He was begrimed with smoke, and the blood trickled down his face from a painful wound an the head, received from the butt-end of a red- skin's musket. A cloth was bound about his head to stanch the blood; otherwise he had escaped serious injury, a lucky thing for us. '3Sandy, oln thap, ' said he, shaking my hand ueartily, aw good-bye, for I fear the worst must come. Nt, amount of bravery can stand against such a host. They swarm like locusts ; each one which falls '•.,ss a dozen ready to follow on. "They will have a tough job to fire this building." Such rites of the Church. as the dangerous position permitted, were solemnly administered by Father Otando. While some women wept, others prayed ; but most of them were inured to danger, and laboured with such good will that my opinion of the opposite sex was changing for the better. "Every man to some specific duty," cried our leader, distributing them as his personal knowledge gauged their worth. "We have no other place of refuge but this and the tower. Your defence will

Ineed coolness and valour, for our only chance is , to hold out as long as possible." 'l,' • t 1 The picture of this last scene is indelibly engraven )1 .311 my memory. It was sad to see the dear children • M cryqng or paralysed with fear, and the heroism of •) the mothers, as they divided their time between attempts to pacify their little ones and strengthen our defensive preparations, was beyond my powers of description. Bluster tells before the world, but tne patience possessed by this little band of women, stricken with dire misfortune, won our admiration. The :wounded groaned as much from a sense of their inability to help us, as from the pain they : endured. One dear, little girl about four years of ' age had her arm broken, and the wistful look on .. her face made my heart ache. That man is not fit ' to live who can be callous to the suffering of babes, . of whom several were .at the breast. No sight ! comes so vividly to my mind as that of a young mother amongst us, who clasped a dead babe to her bosom with mad determination, and. distracted us, ' with her ravings. Her husband was slain at the , beginning of the fight, and in a paroxysm of frenzy ..° she had let the child fall. Concussion of the brain . • ensued, and shortly the little one was a corpse.' j At a later stage the crazy mother rushed out with tO A sortie, and mercifully fell at the hands of the Indians. • When every precaution had been taken, Mark said: "Father Otando, commit us-all to the mercy ' of God." \ The 'good priest prayed earnestly for Divine helg in our extremity, audit is no exaggeration to state ~ that every sane person within those walls was more l resigned to the fate that apparently awaited us. I Shortly, Mark embraced Lucia, and I caught this 1 significant conversation. "Is every preparation made for the end?" he 1 ' asked. "Rest assured of that," replied the Senorita" 1 "but such women as care to face capture have s •. free choice. Some are consoled that an oppor- tunity is given them to pass away in sleep rather than fall into the hands of a relentless foe. Fos ~. ) myself, I ask no further favour-of the Holy Virgin ' in this life, than to die with you." They kissed one another, and I was greatly . impressed by this farewell. Mark did not think it Possible for our little band to hold out long ' against such fearful odds. • The awful significance of his conversation with Lucia dawned on us when one hysterical woman, dreading another melee, and, perhaps, picturing to her distraught mind torture and death by the red- men, drank "the milk of herbs" before the final struggle began. Creeping away from the general gaze, and lying down upon some sacking, she closed her eyes, to wake where the strife of men can never come and the sad at heart have race. Such a circumstance cast a gloom over the com- pany, but our imminent peril was soon too apparent for vain regrets. At first the dusky warriors • .I, feared to approach us, for the blaze from the „11 burning outhouses cast such a glare that we easily a picked off the venturesome. Sparks flew dangerously close to our citadel. The dry wood blazed and crackled around us; and the flames of the burning granaries rose high into the clear sky. What cared Diuro—if he led—that humanli food perished, so that his vengeance could be wreaked on innocent and guilty alike! Fortunately for us; the, gentle breeze carried, the , smoke clear of our rendezvous, and at first the only opportunity the Indians had was to snatch " burning wood and, from some hiding place, throw it at the Presidio. Happily the walls were•mas- sive, and if we could only prevent the enemy getting on the roof and tearing off the tiles the con- flagration around might attract settlers. There was the possibility, too, of our own contingent being nearer than we anticipated ; but alas! the .. chance of succour was so remote that we antici- pated nothing short of the dawn of day could afford us the slightest hope of relief. THE MAYORALTY OF WARRINGTON.

A caucus meeting of the Warrington Town Council was held in the Council Chaniber,at the Town Hell,on Friday night,to consider the question of the Mayoralty and the aldermanic vacancies. At the outset the chair was occupied 137 Alderman Sutton, and subsequently by Aldermeil Hutchinson.lt was moved by Councillor Smothurett seconded by Alderman Roberts that Alderman .I,* E. Wright, J.P., be requested to accept the office of Mayor for the ensuing year. The proposition wss unanimously carried. It is very probable that Alderman Wright will accede to the request. -A MANCHESTER MAN'S LIVING DEATH.

Work is the means by which we live, and when, from whatever cause, employment fails, thousands feel the pinch. Sickness makes countless thousands idle. Some linger long in ill-health, others by taking advantage of means of help quickly recover. One of these last is Mr. John Brownbill, of No. 3, Matlock-place, Chorlton-en- Modlock, Manchester.

His story, as told 'to a press representative—and vouched for by members of his family—is as follows : "I was taken ill over three years ago somewhat sud- denly. I had most severe pain in the stomach and back, caused by kidney disease, and it gave riso to awful suffering. I had a medical man then I was attended by a herbalist, but without cure. Weak after week went by, and I took dozens of bottles of medicine and hundreds of pills but only got worse. I lost my appetite, and what I did eat did me do good. I got gradually weaker, and then I began to suffer from pains in my legs, which made walking almost a torture. I gave up doctors, and someone gave me a prescription for pills, which I got made up. Still there was n change, only that I went very thin. I could not tel what ailed me. And so I went on month after month, often wishing I was dead. Then one day, after two years of what I called a living death. I read an account of some cures by Dr. Williams' Pink Pillsfor Pale People. and I determined—although I was tired of medicine—to give them a trial. You may hardly believe me, but it is a fact—and my wife knows it—that when I had taken two boxes I was all right again. It was not a tern- cure either, for this was over twelve mouths ago, and I have not had a day's sick nese

glum. Why though I

is said, is likely to approach a quarter of a million. A part of this sum is of the nature of initial outlay on permanent arrangements, such, for instance, as the provision of a water supply for the several camps. Still, the estimate has undanbtedly been largely exceeded, especially in the matter of trans- port and supplies ;and it is regarded as certain that a cheaper arrangement will be made next year. There is a good deal of grumbling among the men at the extra outlay they have had to incur on the double score of clothing and messing, and this Is one of the least satisfactory features of the autumn campaign, as it is most desirable that the popularity of the manoeuvres should be maintained. There wee a good deal of extravagance among the officers of certain regiments, but that was to some extent optional. Sumptuous tents and lavish hospitality have to be paid for heavily, but even in the bat- talions that tried to rough it on something like actual service conditions the additional expense is set down at not less than .£lO a head. The result Is that to many the "outing" on Salisbury Plain is not an entirely pleasant reminiscence. Some references have been made to the sickness which has broken out among the Guards since their return to England, but it is slight as com- pared with that which prevails amongst the British troops which are at Cairo mid Alexandria on return , from the Soudan. The cases exceed 800 in number and are chiefly of enteric fever or dysentery. The men, who were kept up by the excitement of the march to Omdurman, and perhaps the daily exer- cise, are now suffering from the reaction. and even were they have not fallen victims to the actual disease, it is acknowledged that they are greatly run down. But other causes besides the reaction conspire to preduem this result, and one of them is the very imperfect barrack and hospital accommo- dation provided for a large number of troops. At Alexandria, for example, no ground can be on ained for either barracks or hospitals. With singular short-sightedness, the Government land round Alexandria has been sold to private individuels, just se if it would never be necessary to land a considerable force of English colliers at any time in Egypt. With such an unworkable administra- tive machine as that of Egypt, it is hard to fix the responsibility on the British officers, but it seems to be equally impossible to aequit them altogether. Much speculation prevails among lawyers as to the inspectorship+ of certified reformatories for inebriates which have been created by the Inebri- ates Ace, 1893. and will be filled up by the Home Secretary under the Act. No qualification, legal or medical, is prescribed f,?Trtihoenseefaptnhe officerso,iantesente, and e the number aIEI romanat subject to the Treasury's approval. I may aid that it is not expected that the salaries offered will be large, or that any clros of the type of the Legal and Medical Commissioners in Lunacy will be con- stituted. A mistake was made by the Licensing Commis- sion in omitting to call as a witness the maneger of a certain public.house in the neighbnurhood of Liverpool-street Station It is one of the most in- teresting establishments in London, and although its management should be an instructive study to persons interested in licensing reform. I beliTize it existence is almost unknown. In this carious pub- lic house a customer who enters with e cigar in his mouth is politely ignored. The waiters fail to see him till he ceases smoking. Should he ask for a pint of beer he is informed that he may net have more than half a pint, and should he after drinking his half pint ask for one more he is invited to take a walk and return in half-an-hour. The principle of "one man one drink" is a fundamental rule of the establishment, subject to slight modifi- cations which I have indicated. The house, so far as! know, is not managed in accordance with any particular philanthropic theory.lts rules are merely a matter of business, and from that point of view they are. said to be a success. The customers are mainly working-class people. A large and strong committee has been formed to erect a memorial to Sir George Grey somewhere in the metropolis. The nature of the memorial has not yet been indicated, and may depend npon the amount of funds received. On the committee we meet with such names as those of Mr. Chamberlain, Lord Rosebnry. the Marquis of Dufferin. Lord Sel- borne, the Marquis of Ripon, end a large number of influential colonists, both official and non-official. About the success of the movement there can be no qaestion, but the committee must not be too ambi- tious.

AMONG THE CRANKS.

BY MR. IL WHITHAm.

I think San Francisco must be the home, the natural home, of the Crank. It matters not, how- ever absurd the idea may be, if it is put forth in sufficiently plausible languages it is almost certain to obtain a hearing, and will, in time, form one of the numerous cults that infest this city. Their number here is almost legion, and would certainly task a life time in giving thought to each one of them. _

The spiritualists out number all others, and are as varied in their teachings and as loving, towards each other as our good friends the Christians. We save not yet built a university, but that is only a matter of time, and then we shall be able to indulge In the loving luxury of preaching the fine art of heresy hunting. The spiritualist and the astro- logist seem at times to blend with each other, and ' while the former gives up an etherealized atheism' which happily no one understands, but believes all the same, the latter throws into the caldron, a something we can lean upon, even, if it be noth- ing better than blind fatalism. These two must do a good business, judging from the increase of their " ads "in the daily papers. The next after these comes the Faith Christ who enacts the role of mule to the Christian scientist donkey. What's in a name? Together they fight fever with faith, hate all sanitary laws, claim the Drs, and have no use for chemistry. When a man recovers his health, it generally means a double conversion that is, to health, and Christian science. If there is a failure, it is a proof, not of the fallacy of the faith cure, but the scarcity of his own faith. We have also the magnetic Healer who, with a whole battery of impossibilities, an irresponsible dynamo of absurdities deals out strong doses of electricity. He also believes very strongly in the laying on of bands, and especially so as regards the purse. This brief list by no means covers the ground un- occupied by the Cranks but there are two other Cranks of whom I wish to speak more freely. a It has been often said of late years that the worlde I am here reminded of a recent editorial that appeared in the "Examiner " under the cap- tion" Have we any New Century Dreams "in ranids Passing through peculiar period of un- st, writer doubted, and " much feared that whicohfthe we have not many dreams of a kind that quicken and illumine the mind." The writer of this pulse can Ihave his pulse quickened if he will only tthh "go Wee' I cannot vouch for the illumination his mind m- ay receive, but I can assure him he will find all the enthusiasm needed,to push from incep- tion of life as it appears to make money. I itniointsto m thousandatur i t ya, tnhde opnhei philosophy can fancy the smile that will appear on the faces of many of the "Examiner " readers when the writer I have above quoted closes his article by saying, "the way of salvation is in our men of letters and our divines." This may be true of our men of letters, but of our divines it can in no sense be true, if we must judge of the future by the past. We have in San Francisco a few, a very few 1 men of letters who Rive us good plain honest food for thought, men who miss no opportunity to give us something more than dreams by means of which we may decently ring out the old century and ring in the new. So far this is satisfactory, but we have,in common with other American cities, and, I may say with most large cities of Europe, a large and almost lawless class, I refer to the politician. The term 'politician ' has become simply a by- word. It means neither more nor less than a man who has determined to make his living by using politics for his own base purposes. Debasing and degrading every avenue open to him,he has driven the decent man into the position of being more willing to pay blackmail in the way of taxes than to take his part in either municipal or national goose government. It is easy to imagine that in this deplorable state of things the political crank finds it an easy matter to put his nostrums on the market. Nationalism, Socialism, Communism, and every other 'ism appear in such outrageous garb that the reformer with the least tinge of Conserva- tism calls a halt with an anxious enquiry: as to where he is being led. The Anarchist wisely hides his head by mixing in the crowd, and cautiously putting forth hie views in such garb and disguise as may conceal his murderous intentions. voI have referred to another crank and lam not clear whether lem justified in so placing him. He calls himself a "Philosopher of Life," and issues much literature. The Profe.sor of selfishness" would be a better designation for him. He thinks 0 there is no law in heaven or earth, that man must needs obey get what you can, and when you can, and get it how you may." To get rich is the one idea, and this can only he done by having per- fect faith in yourself. You are to fully understand that you yourself are an instrument chosen by the law of Divine principle to carry out your purpose. You will then realise the Divine principle within you, mastering and gaining dominion over all things. Having gone so far in this new departure of egotism you will fully comprehend all that is meant in "Believe in self." _ . _ . _

It is belief in the fullness of the power of God. Make the man first. Appeal to belief in yourself. Hold this every day. Due appeal is made to Scrip- ture,and our Christian friends find their God made answerable for this new cultivation of selfishness 1. Establish Thou my thoughts, 0 Lord." Bring back my wandering thoughts. We are not the true sons of God until we have attained dominion over all thugs. We must have deminion over the power of money. When this is accomplished the man is made and you may hold yourself in Purpose. This Purpose is your anchor, and everything will I be attached to that purpose. Purpose—Desire-- Prayer, I can and will accomplish my purpose. I suppose belief and faith as in all religious cults is above all things feces. airy. To understand is a secondary matter If you hold the Father-Mother principle, you will produce the Divine Son, or the Trinity, and whatso- ever you do in this principle shall prosper, Desire is your prayer. The desire must be held fully in the silence. Enter into the temple and ask God, fully in the silence. You have not. a desire, but yott Can open the door to it. So far we have dealt aolsly with the first division of the Philosophy of Life, Purpose.We now come to the second principle or concentration. To concentrate is to bring your thoughts into captivity in obedience to your purr- pose. Your thought is your cepital, your stock in trade, your principle, your friend, your stronghold, Your motive power. Concentrate all your, forces,. and you are told that all earth and hell can not pros vail against you. Believe in yourself, and mould it Into determination. Have faith in the truth that "I and the Father are one." Have faith that I have unlimited power. I AM determined to use this power. make friends of the mammon of unrighteousnees. The Kingdom of Heavfn is in everybody. Now you have your eapital, your stock In trade. You affirm, affirm, affirm. I decree and declare it,that this desire, this purpose in the truth. I decree and declare that it is established unto me. Love your business, lave is the fulfillment of the law. Know your man. Draw every mans fire first, or find out his position first The reader will find now that we are getting well along in our worship of the New God, and is prepared for what follows. Hold the lines,' will be master. 4 nyono who wishes, to ride with M 9 must ride behind. Make toed; ,i• gather forces as you no. Never let go long enough for the magnet to atop attricting.

(Secret) Never let another hold the reins, or soon you will be left behind. Be master; never be driven. Having well learned the lesson of which I have given a brief resume, you are now enabled to say to yourself "Mastery. lem success, success is the eiimnx. the fruit. It ja injustice to take less of God's Kingdom than he intended for us, Whatever Ido shall prosper. Assume a virtue that you dO not seem to have, until it becomes your own. The above is the teaching of a large and growing school or American men and women, and if I have dealt with it under the heading of Cranks, it has been be- cause to me it anpearn to he false in every word and line to all the best principles in man. That the teaching is at all peculiar to America is not at all clear, Judging from the constnntly recurring crop of gooies's and guinea pigs, and WA may well ask ourselves the question, where will it lead us to ? A new God has been set up for our worshin. It is not easy to define him. The Passion for money getting, overshadows everything else in life. It

PARALYSED AND BELPLESS.

SAD STORY OF A NEWTON HEATH FARMER'S

WIFE.

During his lifetime Mr. Gladstone often took occa- sion to paint out the vital importanco of a more general adoption of the system of " petite cultnre " in this country. This nrithod of" little farming" affords a means a livelihood to small capitalists which at ones benefits the workers and tne country at large. One of these little farms, devoted to raising noultry and pigs, is known as Old Farm, Dean-tane, Newton Heath, near Manchester. Mrs. Singleton did the mijor portion of the work, early and late, and the little under- taking was prosperous, when one day—on October 9th, 1896—she was streck down as hy a lightning flash with paralysis, and carried unconscious to bed. Medical aid was summoned, but with little effect. For five weeks the sufferer lay unconscious, and when the brain did once again assert its power, she awoke only to find her- self what she expressed as " not Balt the woman she was." The nerves of sensation had lost their tone, and the nerves of motion were little better. As she told a Manchester reporter, she was a wrack. She could not smell or taste, could her very indistinctly, and could only totter about with difficulty.

~ I consulted other doctors, one a man of consiler- able fame," she went on," but all in vain. I got no bettor. Month after month passed, and I was in des- pair. One day in the beginning of this Tear, my soR name in and said, ' Now,tor my sake, mother,try a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. They will do you good.' I refused, but he was so persistent that I got a box, just to pleat° him. By the time I had taken them I was much better. Before that, I had been unahle to get my words out properly,and could not say the words I wanted. But after the first box my epee.% came back. I went on taking; them, and continued tl improve, so that I began to go about like my old self. Now I have taken eight boxes, and I am a new woman; can get about my household work, and, more than that, can look after our stock outside. In abandoning the street collections this year the organisers of the Hospital Saturday Fund have shown a degree of moral courage for which it to be hoped they may be rewarded in hard cash. The street collection in its earlier days was an inoffen- sive and effective means of reaching the pockets of a class of people whe seldom give a thought to the maintenance of the great medical charities of the country. Latterly, however, the system has been seriously abused. All kinds of societies and petty institutions have got into the way of entering the field, their method being to send processions of ex- tremely persistent collectors round the streets of the suburbs, headed by a band and carrying a few banners. Naturally the public were beginning to resent the nuisance, and they have been showing their resentment lately by withholding subscrip- tions even from deserving charities. It is,however, an encouraging sign of the times that, while street collections are falling away in value, the workshopl returns of the Hospital Saturday Fund continue to I yield a yearly increase. Last year the amount real- ised from this source was £16,000, and this year it is likely to be more by some theusende of pounds. The General Committee of the National Liberal Club, by a resolution passed unanimously at the last annual meeting, were instructed to take such steria as they might think best for perpetuating the memory of Mr. Gladstone in the Club. After con- sidering various suggestions,they have come to the conclusion that the improvement of the library, which was founded in Mr. Oladstone's lifetime and opened by him, will beet fulfil this object, and the proposal which they make is that a sum of not less than £4,000 should be raised, to be expended on the development of the library, special care being taken to increase its value for political and historical reference. Mr. Herbert Os intone, M.P., Mr. Henry Gladstone, Sir W.H. Wills,M.P., and Mr.J.P.Thomas- son head the first list of subscriptions with .£5O each (the first three named making their donations con- ditional on not less than £4,000 being raised), and amongst other contributories are Lard Ripon, Sir J. T. Brunner, M.P„ Mr. A. Spicer, M.P., Mr. F. S. Stevenson, M.P., Mr. Clough, M.P., and Mr. Fisher _ _

The smashing of Itandism has been accomplished at a figure considerably below what in contractors' phraseology is called the estimate. It is said to have been done at a quarter of a million. or £300,000 below the expectators of the War °film. I understand that a million and a quarter. or per- haps even less, will cover the whole coat of the campaign, in which respect it compares wonder- fully with the many millions of outlay iecurred on Lora Wolseley's campaign. This reflects the great- est credit on every Egyptian department connected with the Omdurman struggle, and also on the Birder, whose sleepless vigilanee supervieed every- thing. To keep down such vast possibilities of waste as a 'wig desert march afforded implied the most careful plans and foresight, and the most minute arrangement of details, and the economy with which the Mabdi power has be broken up al- most takes the ludicrousness off Mr. Chamberlain's theory of" campaigning on the cheap." Nov, that the.De Rougament exposure is fully before the public, a goodtwiny people are asking what the British Association mean to do to repair the blunder made by the ofilciale of that body in giving the adventurer a scientific cachet. There is no other instance in the records ef the Association of so complete a deception. But, unique though it be, the episode may yet do serious damage to the prosperity and reputation of our greateet intellec- tual congress, The imagined necessity for a " sen- sation" was probably at the root of the mischief. De Rougemont—to give the man his favourir e name —appeared in the held at an opportune moment, and supported as he was by one or two acknow- ledged geographical experts, his narrative and credentials were perhaps less closely scrutinised than they ought to have been. I believe his spon- sors are of opinion that the case might be charitably regarded as one ln which the least said tho soonest mended. On the other hand, a really strong feeling exists in favour of some action on the part of the British Association by which the blunder, as far as possible, should be publicly ree'ified. The battle of Omdurman showed that continuous and rapid firing impairs the effectiveness of the Lee-Metford rifle. It was reported at the time that the barrel became BO hot that the men in the fight- ing line found it. almost impossible to hold their weapons. Now it is said that the same cause affec- ted in a slight degree the accuracy of the rifle. Of course inquiry is to be made, and a series of experi- ments is proiected,with a yiow to seeing how many rounds can be fired before accuracy falls off. GENERAL NEWS.

The Prince of Wales has definitely determined not to attend any raee meeting during the present year. Lady Cromer died soon after sunset on Sunday evening at Cairo. The deceased lady wss the daughter of Sir Rowland Stanley Errington, Bart. A fishing boat was captized off Dorsey Island. Berehaven. One of the six occupants—a young man of 25, named Peter O'Leary—was drowned, The police raided a club in Shaftesbury Avenue, London on Tuesday afternoon, and about a hundred persons were conveyed to the police station in batches in a pantechnicon van. At Carnarvon, on Saturday, the Carnarvonshire confirming , Justices refused to confirm the new license granted to the Gogarth Abbey Hotel, Llandudno, by the magistrates of the ' Conway division. The funeral of the late Queen of Denmark took place on Saturday afternoon in the ancient cathedral of Roskilde. where many of the sovereigns of Denmark lie buried. The ceremonies were simple, but touching. Seven Mussulmans, sentenced to death by the British Court-martial, were hanged at Candle on Tuesday—three for the murder of two British soldiers outside the town,and four for the murder of patients at the British hospital. A terrific gale prevailed on Tuesday at different points of the British coast. The Norwegian barque Urra was wrecked on Tuesday morning on the Northumberland coast, seven of the crew being saved and two drowned. Three fishing boats have been broken. A Norwegian barque was driven ashore on Tuesday at Kilnsea, and owing to the heavy seas went to pieces. So far as could be ascertained only two et the crew were saved by the rocket apparatus. and it is feared that the whale of the remainder have perished. A great fire occurred in the petroleum port at Astrakhan. caused by the bursting of the boiler of a steam pump. Two steam pumps, seven barges, and three hundred poods of naphtha residuum were destroyed. Three men are reported killed, and several injured. At the Liverpool Police Court on Saturday, a young man named Martin Connor, was sentenoed to four months' hard labour for stealing a watch and chain from Miss Harriet Husker, in Byrom-street, and a month's additional imprison- ment for assaulting the police. A serious encounter tonic place at Burnmouth. Berwick- shire, on Saturday afternoon, between the water bailiffs and fishermen, arising out of the serving of summonses for the alleged illegal capture of salmon. Several people, including a number of women, were severely injured. A cyclist named Williamcemployed as a carrier by Messrs. Debenham and Freeborly, was turning into Oxford-street from Tottenham Court-road, London. when he came into collision with an omnibus. He was knocked down by the horses, and run over. He died within a few minutes. At the Essex Quartet' Sessions on Wednesday two fellows named Williamson and Hook, bargemen. were eenteneed to eve years penal servitude for a savage assault on a young girl. The assault was committed in the presence of several London labourers, who made no attempt to protect the poor girl. H.M.S. first-class crueler Argonaut, launched in January last from the yard of the Fairfield Company, Glasgow, began her speed trials on Monday on the Clyde. Proceeding down the Channel on Saturday, a slight explosion took place in the engine-room, five stokers being scalded, one somewhat seriously. It was ascertained on Monday !night on the arrival of the Calais-Paris mail train at Calais that during the journey the Dowager-Duchess of Sunderland's despatch box ,containing jewellery of the estimated value of £30.000, had been ab- stracted from the compartment in which she and Sir Allman Holllt were travelling. INSPECTION OF THE OAS WORKS BY THE CORPORATION.

It will to remembered that a short time ago a visit was paid to tee Corporation Gas Works by the Manches- ter and Dietrict Association of Gas Engineers. It was afterwards suggested that the members of the Council should be allowed a similar privilege, and the. Chairman of the Gas Committee (Alderman Fairclough) accordingly invited them. The visit was paid last Thursday, and we understand that the members of the Council came away satisfied with the imr revetments welch hay' been made. A short description of the processes nt gas manufacture at the work?, prepared by Mr. Haddock, gas engineer, I was handed to each member present. 'The company included the Mayor, most of the members of the Coun- cil, with Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr. Haddock (gas engineer), Mr. Chandley (assietant engineer), Mr. Taylor (collector), and Mr. It. T. Fairclough (formerly Chairman of the Gas Committee). We extract the following particulars from the des- cription furnished by Mr. Haddock : "With the new retort settings, known as Klonne's Regenerative Gaseous Firing, the coke is c )(averted into gas. The gases are burned with heated air in direct contact with tee out- side of the retorts. The air is heated by passieg the spent gases through flues,between which are sandwiched the air ducts for conveyine the hot air iato the setting. By travelling the .veaste gases towards the chimney, and the air in a contrary direction, it becomes heated, and the waste gases cooled. The coksaving effected by the regenerated fire,as corn. pared with direct firirmis over 30 per cent. in our own cast we estimate it at £3OO to £5OO per annum. As ex- plained by the Deputy-Chairman et the September Council meeting, the Gas Committee decided to recom- mend the introduction of West's Manual stoking ma- chinery. By the use of this machinery GI, men are saved the lifting of the coal, and the weight of the tools is borne by the machinery. The lab mous work is done by stearn,and it will enable us to reduce the number of workers exposed to the heat of the retort house. The gas, having been thoroughly purified, is passed on to the meter, where it is measured,and the hourly make of gas registered by the foreman stoker. A j t photo- meter is placed near the meter, which gives a rough indication of the illuminating poem-,and the proportion of cannel used is regulated to give a quality of 18 candles. The accuracy of the jet photomet .r,al ne with the illuminating power of the gas, is tested three times daily On a Lethby Bunsen bar photeenet.ir, which is coaled and stamped by the Beard of Trade. The gas is then stored in the gasholdere. When all the holders (two at Longford and three at Mersey-street) are full the gas thee. contain will ripply the town for a little more than 21 hours. The wnole ot the processes of gas an anutacture having been goats through there remains the dietri nation of the gas and regulation of the pressure; this is done by means of a piece of apparatus called the governor, which consists of a floating drum, to which is attacho i a cone or throttle valve; this regulates the pressure from the gas hollers, and by mewls of water, which is run on or off the governor to increase or decrease the pressure as may be required, a regular and sufficient pressure is maintained on the ontstanding districts, which of course means a higher pressure in the town, as the fectios on the pipes produces a diminution in toe am ount of prase sore. An automatic pressure register is the governor house marks on a paper the exact amount of pressure given at any hour of the day." At the conclusion of the inspection an adjeiirement was made to the engineer's office where tight refresh- ments were served. The Mayor expres..e 1 the gratifica- tion the visit had afforded him. He was struck by the excellent order of the works and was pleased with the improvements which were being made. Great praise was due to Mr. Haddock and his assistant for the work they were doing. H concluded by proposing the health of the Chairman and Decuty-Cheirm an of the Gas Committee.—/ilderman Fairclough, and Mr. Robert eaton responded. Mr. RICHARD Fsisaceotem in the course of a short speech related the many difficulties he had to contend with at the Gas Works when he was Chairman et the Committee. The works had been pulled out of a state of chaos to one of complete order. A great saving had been effected in many departments and this had enabled them to carry out the alterations without having re- course to the revenue of the concern or th i rates. Mr. Haddock had been severely handicappe I by the condition In which the works were lei t him hut he fel'', that in a short time the works would be second to noes in the kingdom. The MAYOR proposed the health of Mr. Haddock in complimentary terms. In responding Mr. HADDOCIL said be was glad the Council had visited the work ; as they would now be able to appreciate the difficu ties which they ha l had to contend with. He had always tried to keep up the standard of the gas to 18 candles. In con- clusion he thanked the members for their appreciation of his services.

Tin conflict between the United The Debt Cuban States and Spain about the Cuban debt is somewhat complicated. The facts of the case are• very simple, however, and they may as well be stated. The debt on the island amounts to seventy millions sterling. Of I I this sum the island owes ten millions to Spain. The debt is held by Cubans, by Spaniards, anti only to a very limited extent, it is thought, by foreigners. Of course, as a whole, it represents the money expended in modern times in putting down chronic rebellion, rather than in developing the resources of the island. If the United States had conquered Cuba for purposes purely Ameri- can, and were intending to govern the island as a conquered country the natural course of things would be for the debt ,to go with the island; though it may be remem- bered that the Federal Government, after the Civil war in the United States, repudiated all responsibility for the Confederate debt. The American Government now takes the sound posi- tion that it does not aspire to rule Cuba as part of the 'United States, and it is not intending to accept its sovereignty. Cuba has been freed from Spanish rule, and the Cubans have got to rule themselves. Spain, on her part, says that her authority over the island is gone, that henceforth it will not be in her power to draw a peseta of revenue from the place, and that hence to saddle her with the Cuban debt is unjust. There ought • to be some equitable way out of these entanglements. If it be unfair to cast the debt on America entirely; it is equally unfair to leave it on Spain. In other cases where countries have been freed the general course has been for the countries to contribute towards the debt which had formerly rested upon them. This is an equitable arrangement, and it helps us in the present case. The Cubans must have imposed upon them a certain portion of the debt now resting upon the island. How much it is not necessary to determine oflhand. Spain must con- tinue to bear a portion also. But is America to wholly escape ? That is the crux of the difficulty. We can see no way of solving the matter unless PRESIDENT McICINLEv admits that the United States Government does assume some kind of overlordship over the island, amount- ing to a Protectorate. If we were asked to make a fair financial adjustment of the debt, we should divide it into five parts, and contend that Cuba herself should take ever three-fifths— that is, forty-two millions—leaving Spain and the United States to pay fourteen millions each, or divide up the twenty-eight millions in some other agreeable way. Unless some plan of this kind can be devised,or if the debt be saddled almost entirely lon Spain, it will be unpaid, it will be altogether lost. The Cubans will be in a strong financial position after a time. The island is wealthy, its resources are abundant, and its trade and com- merce will go up by leaps and bounds as soon as internal tranquillity and economical administration are assured. Hence the island ought to take over the major portion of its own debt, and proceed to pay it off. The work will be considerable, but it is by no means impossible. Liberty is often costly to begin with, and it would indeed be unjust [to [Spain if she lost the island and had to go on pay- mg for the big debt upon it.

THE Emrsnou of GERMLNY Pilgrims and Bombs. as a pilgrim to tho Holy Land

is an interesting figura. Cynics may scoff and politicians may sinilo, but to

the general public there must be some attraction

in a visit as singular as it seems to be puzzliug. The laisEn is a restless creature, and he has per- turbed Europe more than once by his freaks upon

religious and social questions. He is now bent upon making a pil4rimage which must, in the nature of things, be enveloped in some consider- able mystery. Au Emperor is an enthusiast if he

pleases, audit would be hard to deny to him the liberty of action and thought claimed by the humblest Christian. But when he designs to make visits of the kind just commenced he forces the rest of the world to find some other motives than those which animated a pious Lutheran towards the end- of the nineteenth century. He has managed to enlist upon his side the SULTAN, who regards his 'visit as an act of friendship which may be of service hereafter. The Turks in Palestine and

its neighbourhood are full of enthusiasm, we are told, because a Christian Emperor is going to visit the sites and shrines held in special reverence by Christians, whom they generally designate as in- fidels. Of itself this is a very remarkable state of things, and it may well puzzle Western minds. Whether the services rendered by Germany to the SULTAN, to the Turkish army, and to the Turkish cause in recent troubles in Greece have had any- thing to do with this enthusiasm can only be guessed. It may be enthusiasm made to order, and as evanescent as this kind of popular fervour usually is. But that it is being manifested it hardly seems possible to doubt. The whole country is being prepared for a visit which must be even more unintelligible to the Turks of Syria than it is to the Christians of Europe. TIIO advance to Jerusalem will be a kind of triumphal pro• cession, and the stay there a groat religious event. Is the KAISER about to pr oclaim himself the custodian of the Holy Places, conjointly with the Turk ? Is he about to enter into some occult alliance with the SULTAN? Is he developing plans , antagonistic to Russia? Or is he simply visiting , the Holy Land as an individual Christian, a pious, Emperor, an inspired potentate? It may be that ' he will make declarations of interest on some of these points before or directly after he returns. The Anarchists are indifferent to these aspects of the visit. They have only seen in it one more chance of ridding the world of an Imperial personage Had the KAISER gone to Cairo there were desper- ate men there who would have made an attempt to kill him. They were to take a house in the capital and throw bombs at him as he passed by. He probably became acquainted with their designsf and changed his plans. The Anarchists tried to get to Palestine,and an attempt was to be made in Jerusalem. Tho Anarchists seem to be all Italians, and not Germans. They have no special political grievances against the KAISER. But they are at war with kings and queens, as representing law, order, and the existing constitution of things. For the time they have been defeated, but it is probable that some of the conspirators are already in Palestine, and if so we may still have some - sational events. An

seri' 'U'

influential German paper says "Abdo Harald, in spirit and in character, has not a few' points in common with Kaiser Wilhelm the second." One's first thoughts on reading this ex- traordinary comparison of potentates is that the writer stands a very good chance of being put in prison for Pse majeste--Emperor William's fav- ourite weapon when punishing too outspoken pressmen. But the facts of the matter are rather different than might be supposed. The journal quoted, has so suited itself to the occasion—the Emperor's visit to Turkey—as to devote a couple of columns to a panegyric on the Sultan. Our readers will boa little suprised to learn, from thig source, that the Sultan of Turkey "is wise and thoughtful, he has introduced remark. able judicial reforms into Turkey, built asylureS for the poor, hospitals for the sick, and is collo corned for the education of his people. He 10 also built thousands of miles of railway, and it is only ignorance or malice that could represent the' Sultan as opposed to reform. Those who kilos; 0 him know how consumed he is with reforming' zeal. He is a terribly hard worker, finding hi only relaxation in the bosom of his family. ilea, • is gentle, friendly, sympathetic, benevolent, toler' ' ant,good,magnanimous, liberal,and impulsive."AnC then the article concludes with comparing theKaiset,, to this "saint on earth." Note the nine adjen't tives in the last sentence of the quotation. Abdul Hamid has not even the faults and frailties tbst all flesh is heir to, in the opinion of the con- scienceless writer of this absurd "puff." Solt often have we had proofs incontrovertible of the Sultan's villany, deceit, craft and cruelty ? The whole world knows the harrowing tale of the Armenian atrocities and the Sultan's share therein. " Yet there is a newspaper in the Fatherland 00 subservient to officialdom, so anxious to currY favour with the Emperor, and above all so devoid of truthfulness and honour as to laud to the skies the despotic and debased monarch whom Glad" atone fitly described as the "Great Assassin."

-- Developments in the adjuncts to warfare are rapid and ceaseless, and the Czar's disarmament • proposal has had no different effect. An evening; paper published a paragraph this week alluding to' the now famous rescript, and immediately above it particulars of active war preparations in France. , The irony of the two paragraphs was probably 110"xt intentional, and therefore all the keener. On ear side the Channel there is no relaxation of the endeavour to keep our sea footing on an equiva.• lent with that of any other two powers. Each battleship that is launched seems to surpses, her predecessors in offensive and defensive equir ment. The Goliath, the latest to leave the stocklio when fitted with her full armament, will be able to project shot at the rate of seven tons per minute. The Inflexible was a marvel in her dal and the tremendous shells she threw into the forts of Alexandria were quite sufficient to account for the demoralisation of the Egyptian gunners. Bat she is antiquated in comparison with the Goliath, whose citadel is to all intents and purposes ire' penetrable, and whose heaviest projectiles are 850 lbs weight. Truly, universal peace appears hePe" lessly distant, Lut the need for an understanding tantamount to such a happy state of existence is plainly very great.
