Nevertheless two mower, ,missing in consequence. The disaster here w 8,5 remarkably like the one at Latchford Locks, when the Harold rammed the flood-gates and nearly, emptied the upper reaches of the Canal. The steamer , Blanche Rock sought admission to the Morpeth dock, and under the in- fluences of a boisterous tide . and perhaps some fatal miscalculation of speed. she dashed, against the outer barrier and tore' the gates from their hinges. An eight-teet *all of water immediately ' aWsipt,t42 Blanche Rock into the middle of the , and.siiiiilarrY broke- from their moorings about- half-a-dozen flats ; a .pontoon, atid one or two large cargo boats stationed in the dock also tlapped- thtfir hawsers when .the water- so sud- idenly left their keels. Then, on the same day, an awful catastrophe befel an express train at Wrawby kinction, cadaing the loss of nine lives. Travelleri were dozing, -chatting, or reading in fancied secirity when the carriages were splintered and unroofed by some timber-baulks projecting from a derailed waggon. The Great Central Rail- Way Company have much to answer for in this in- stance. Such a calamity might happen any time Were not the utmost precautions taken, and Whether all due care and thoughtfulness was ex- ercised at Wrawby Junction seems at least open to doubt. Finally, as if misfortune had not been the cause of sufficient horror, the gale adds more calamities to the list. The north east coast is dotted with wrecks, one of which at Redcar has resulted in the drowning of twelve men. The papers, this week, have literally teemed with an accumulation of horrors arising from unforseen catastrophes of great magnitude. " Troubles," we are told "never come singly," and this time they have arrived in batches sufficient to appal the stoutest heart.

The Rev. H. G. Roberts has not been in War- l'ington very lot*, but he has discovered that one uf the worst features of the towtr—is the state of Sankey-street on Sunday evenings. Preaching at a children's service on Sunday, the rev, gentleman alluded in terms of warmth and sarcasm to what he called "the Sabbabcal oromenade " in Sankey- Street, at the same time exhorting the younger luernbers of his congregation to shun such a Illethod of spend:ng Sunday evening. Mr. Roberts la not the first gentleman whose soul has been stirred to wrath at the senseless promenad- illg of Sankey-street on the evening of the Sabbath. Not only is the thoroughfare greatly obstructed—notwithstandincr the efforts of the police to prevent loitering and larking "-- but the spectacle of so many boys and girls,voung leen and young women, idling away their time in loch childish fashion is a sad one. The reflection Itthrows on the parents of Warrington families is aerious. They cannot be ranch concerned about their children's moral welfare, if they allow them 40 desecrate the Sabbath by participating in the rowdyism and inanity which still goes on in tlaukey-street during and after the hours of even- 14g worship. We hope Mr. Roberts's timely !ords will be carefully noted and acted upon by those who heard them, and possibly Sankey-street taa3' profit simultaneously with the younger gener- elios. AtanENT ra'r OLD QUAY WORKS. On Thursday morning what may be characterised as a narrow escape from death, occurred to a man named Joseph Buckley, who resides at Dover-street. It appears that Buckley was engaged in the production of what is known as " Blue Billy "and whilst crossing the vats was observed to totter, and had he not been assisted by Joseph Richardson, and another fellow worker, would have fallen and certa'nly lost his life. Buckley, who has been attended by Dr. McDougall, now lies in bed at his residence suffering from pains in ths back. It is sup- mooed thkt be was tot,ined with a fit of some kind, but as he is not known to have been siibiict to such attacks and as be is dumb, the point is not perfectly clear. AN OBSTRUCTIVR RUNCORN BILLOCK. A cow-catcher would have been a useful attachment to the engine of the Liverpool express at Runcorn on Tuesday evening. A bullock, belonging to Mr. Bell, butcher, strayed from afield and jumped on the railway between Sutton and Runcorn. The animal defied the advance of tee expreis, and the driver stopped the train In order to prevent an accident. The railway officials were occupied ten minutes in driving the animal to a place of safety, when the train proceeded. The unusual incident greatly interested the paseengere.

WOUNDING CASE AT WIDNES. _ . .

At Widnes Police Court on WedoesdaY. -before Mr. B. Brown, a middle-aged woman named Elizabeth Bushell, 4 Pool-street, Wag charged with unlawfully wounding a man named John Grady on Tuesday afternoon. The parties had been drinking nearly all day, and a quarrel ensuing between Bushell and Grady, the former struck prosecutor violently on the head with a teapot,inflicting three wounds, one just above the left temple an inch long and penetrating to the bone. When charged the prisoner replied, " I did it in self-defence."—She was committed to the Liverpool Assizes for trial. WITH THE " FICKWICKIANS." Tuesday night saw a good number of the " Pick wick- lane " assembled at the Brunner Guild Hall, under the presidency of Mr. George Hapes. The speaker for the evening was Mr. W. Millington, whose subject was " Errata."—Following the reading of the paper there was open discussion in which Messrs. T. Sanders, T. Millinger, Davirs, Bennion, and Williams took part.— At the close of the proceedings the Chairman compli- mented the members of the club on the instruction, and diversity of topic they provided, concluding with the remark that the meeting was one of the best held under the auspices of the society for some time.

Tag Sorrs or TEMPERANCE. The usual meeting of the above Order was held on Saturday evening in the Greenway-road Schoolroom, under the presidency of Mr. Edward Key, jun. Liter the ordinary business had been transacted, Mr. Thomas Cooke, one of the auditors, gave his report for the past quarter. He stated that during tho quarter they had lost one member by death, and two wives of members, £32 having been paid out of the funeral fund of the district. They now had 285 members, and about £2O had been paid into the central fund during the quarter. The cadets were in a very flourishingcondition, number- ing 84, and were increasing week by week. The funds amounted to £ll9 163. A number of important sugges- tions were made by Mr. Cooks which would make the Order more efficient. The following officers and mem- bers took part in the meeting :—Messrs J Noden, Thomas J Holt, Thomas Whyte, Daniel Bisbrown, George Holt, James Johnson, and John Jones.

WIDNES SCHOOL BOARD. At the monthly meeting of this Board on Tuesday, Florence Bennett (West Bank),John Carter (Warrington- road), Maggie Burrows, and Arthur Nickson (Sinam's Cross) were awarded scholarships at the Higher Grade School and School of Science. Miss Gerrard was ap- pointed teacher of cookery at a salary of £BO per annum. The financial statement for the half-year ended the 29th September last was submitted. It showed that the total receipts other than loans amounted to £9,6911. 18s. sd. The total expenditure was £7,387 10s. id. The outstand- ing liabilities of the Board amount to £29,837 17s. 9d. The balance sheet was approved. Her Majesty's Inspector of Schools reported that the Higher Grade School had been so successful in attracting scholars that the present temporary accommodation was in many respects inade- quate, and the Board should lose no time in providing new buildings, with suitab'e playgrounds and offices. The report was referred to the School Management Com- mittee, the Chairman remarking that the school had evidently been too successful. BOYS' BRIGADE CHURCH PARADE. The firat parade of the session of the Runcorn Wesleyan Boys' Brigade, took place on Sunday morning last, and proved very successful. Four companies took part viz.. Camden, 3 officers 30 rank and file; Brunswick. 3 officers, 38 rank and file ; Nelson-street, 1 officer, 10 rank and file ; and Halton, 2 officers, 25 rank and file. The Runcorn Brigade assembled in Camden schoolroom, from whence they marched, preceded by the Weston Wesleyan Brass Band, by way of Church-street, High-street,and Bridge. street to meet the Halton Brigade, returning by way of Bridge-street, and High-street to St. -Paul's Wesleyan , Chapel. Here a spccial sermon was preached by the Rev. C.W.L. Christien (chsplain of the Brunswick Com- pany). The Brigade was commanded by Captain J. W. Woodland,and the other officers present were Captains T, Leach (jun), Marsh, Smith (Halton), Lieutenants Gittins, Littlemore, Holt, and Mulkey (Halton). In- structor Rushton was eleo in attendance.

AN INTERESTING ESTABLISHMENT. —ViSitots to Man. chegter can hardly fail to be imprassed with the tine establishment now occupied by Samuel Pitts in that city. This princely building which is magnificently fitted up and lighted by electricity covers 9 900 square feet of ground, has a storage caracity of 1,000,000 feet, and is five storeys in height. Unlike many businesses which of mushroom growth, often as quickly disappear, Pitts's emanated from a small building in Brook-street about 50 years ago. Since thon they have occupied nremises in Gaythorn. Their progress has been step by step to the pe3ition they now occupy. Wh,3n one con- siders that fact the eld saying recurs to one's mind that t' there is always room at the top." The rapid strides made by this great firm of complete house, office, and shop furnishers is due to their admirable business methods. They have stu lied the public on all points. Quality and moderate price are the chief factors in the success of their business. They have mastered that happy combination, and the visitor to their establish- ment can to assured of being attended to courteously ; and in addition there is every convenience for the cus- tomer including hydraulic passenger and luggage lifts.

E EX IN Ett FRI AY, OCTOBER . 21, 1898

It'UNCORN NOTES.

[BY A. CORRESPONDENT]

The opening ceremony, in connection with the Runcorn Free Library, will take place on Wednes- day next, and will be performed by Sir John Brunner, Bart., M.P. To make the ceremony as imposing as it should be is a task that perplexes the Library Committee to some extent. Considering the general popularity of Sir John Brunner, there is sure to be a large crowd of interested people present to witness the proceedings—hence the trouble—how are they going to be accommodated ? No one seems to know, and like a good many more problems of a similar kind, it will probably have t 3 solve itself. But, after all, the people who are interested in the Free Library will not, mind the crush on this occa- sion,

A good many young fellows who are at a loss to know where to spend a pleasant and instractive evening, might "go further and fare worse,* than pay a visit to the haunt's of the • PAckwick Club. which meets in thb Brunner Guild liall.lt is just the place where a younteman who possesses ideas and thinks he can • talk' ought to gym There is much knbwledge and no little amusement to be obtained at very small cost.

Like a "bolt from the blue," has fallen upon the people in Runcorn and district a succession of calamities which. bring:in their wake sorrow and mourning—but which help to kindle in our breats that feeling which makes all 'the world kin.' Com- mencing with the sudden death of Mr. S. Yarwood, the death of Mr. Coleburn, the death 'ot Corporal .James Gibbs (in Egypt); and the sudden suffoca- tion of the little girl Louisa Graham,the local death roll is added to by a calamity due to the bursting of the Morpeo Dock gates at Birkenhead, entailing great damage to property and the loss of two lives— those of highly respected Runcorn flatmen. Thus, truly can it be said that the fates are dealing hardly with Runeorn bon at home and abroad.

"In things musical it is satisfactory to observe that the Runcorn Philharmonic Sniety seem to have breastei the wave of trouble with which they had to.contend. and now gaily ride upon its crest. Their conductor is, undoubtedly, an acquisition. The Rhondda Valley Glee Party Nadi visit Runcorn to perform in aid of the Runcorn Volunteer Band, and no one should lie the opportunity of hearing them. Their singing reminds all who hear it Of the lines : " Angela choirs from on high, . Their heavenly anthems in the sky"."* The Orchestral Society are providing diversity of entertainment in the way of smokers,' at which a pleasant musical evening is Obtained at popular prices.

a tendency becoming more general amongst employers to be considerate and humane. They regretted that all their great employers of labour had left the neighbour- hood—and left without leaving anything behind them, bat disused works. They wanted people who would conduct their works on the same lines as the Cotner Kellner Company.—That was with consideration to their workpcople. (Hear, hear ) De. McDougall seconded, and said, that in his capacity as a public officer he had the passing of about 100 to 120 children -every year who were about to launch oul intp the world of employment, and he was struck with the fact that the majority of them had only just passed the 4ta standard. He would press upon parents the responsibility that rested upon them in that respect. • Dr. McDougall paid a high compliment to the Caster Kellner Company, and to Mr. Mather in particu- hr, amid referred to the time when the works of the latter g,rentlemun were peacefully conduct-d, owing to the considerate treatment of the workmen during the ter:ible times accompanying the late engineer's strike. He he artily secanded the vole, which was a-....eorded with acelaination. Mr. W. Mather • responded, and thanked the vicar, aching that he itiorrd that the rev, gentleman would inform him how his cotleagues and himself could best ' serve the interests of the town and district in an un- o:tentatious niaririei for helcduld assure them as soon as they—who were practically a new indastry—had be- come fairly established, they would , not be toehind hand in contributingtheir share to the common welfare of Runcorn and its people. --- TEIE FERRY QUESTION. The quarterly mseting of the Thelwall Parish Council was hold in the Thelwall Schools on Monday evening. Admiral Jones-Parry, in the absence of the Vicar, pre- sided. There were also present : Messrs. Bleckly, Roe- buck, Kite,Warburton, Taylor, Ford, Houghton, and W. B. Cook (clerk). The Clerk said that in accordance with instructions, he had written to Messrs. Greenall and Bucktso, solici- tors, Warrington, for legal advice as to who was respon- sible for any accident at the ferry after 9 p.m., and whether the ferryman was obliged to take anyone over after 9 p.m. In answer, the solicitors stated that the Ship Canal Company were liable for any accident, and that the ferry could be worked after 9 p.m. if re- quired. Mr. Bleckly said it was a qusstion now whether they should leave the matter ia the hands of tne District Council or go on with it themselves. The Chairman pointed out the difficulty of arousing the ferryman at night when one happened to be on the opposite side of the canal. Mr. Bleakly said they must do the best they could. They had a right to cr3sl the ferry,and if the ferryman did not come they should make a com. pla..int. about it. . .

The Chairman said if people would tell the ferryman what time they would come back he would be there to take them across. Mr. Blackly moved that the whole of the correspond- ence be handed to the District Council for them to deal with. The motion was seconded by Mr. Roelsuck, and Carried. " • " •

Mr. Blackly said that at the last meeting it was unani- mously agreed that something ought to be done to the village stream, which wag not in a creditable condition. He proposed that the Clerk be instructed to obtain speci- fications and tenders for the cleansing of it, and the paving up to the margin in a suitable manner. It would not be a costly undertaking, and it would make the brook rather an ornament than otherwise. The water would run away quickly, and the rubbish would not accumulate, as it did present. _ .

, The Chairman stated that the water would not be fit for cattle to drink then. Mr. IK,te thought that a pipe laid down to take the sewage out of the brook before the cleansing would be a better plan. The water would then be fit for cattle to drink. The motion was not seconded. Mr. Taylor thon proposed that the water be piped up and that the place be levelled with tts road. Mr. Roebuck seconded the motion. . .

—..--- The Chairman moved, as an amendment that the brook he narrowed go tat thero would h 3 a stream of water always running. The motion was seconded by Mr. Sleekly. Neither motion was carried. _

On the motion of Mr. Sleekly the meetinz was ad- journed for a month, so that tenders and further infor- mation could be obtained.

It is with regret that we announce the death of Mr. James Rhoales Buckton, formerly a solicitor prictisin?, at Warrington, which event took place at is residence, Hollins Hey, New Brighton, en Tuesday afternoon. The deceased gentleman. who was 74 years of age, had a stroke some years ago, but until Tuesday went about much as usual,when he became unconscious. His medi- cal attendant, Dr. Craignoile, was sent for, but his ser- vices were of no avail, and Mr. Buckton passed away shortly after his arrival. Mr. Bucktem came to War- rington in 1868, when he bucame the second psrtner in the firm of Messrs. Marsh, Buckton, and Jeans, and on Mr. Marsh's retirement he was appointed joint clerk with the late Mr. James Nicholsun to the magistrates of the County Petty Sessional Division (Warrington and Newton). On the death of Mr. Nicholson Mr. Buckton was appointed sole clerk, an office he held until his retirement on the 31st December, 1890, when he was succeeded by Mr. Henry Greenall, the head of the firm of Messrs. Greenall and Buckton, solicitors, Winwick-at. The deceased was also for many Nears clerk to the Laud Tax and Income Tax Commissioners—a poslt,on now held by his son, Mr. J. Douglas Buukton. Toe deceased was a honorary membir of the Committee of the Infirm. ary, an institution in which at one time he manifested considerable interest. He leaves a sv,claw, two sons (Mr. J, D. Buckton and the Rev. Harold Buckion,of Wybun- lbar9),and two daughters.

A CHILD'S SAD DEATH AT RUNCORN.

CHOKED BY A PIECE OF BRED.

' At the Runcorn Police Court, on Tuesday morning, Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, held an inquest on the body of Louisa Graham,aeed 4 years, daughter of Mi. Thomas Cowan Graham, Eilis'-court, Bridgewater-street, Run. corn,who died from suffecattoa on Friday last at about 330 o'clock, by reason of a piece cf bread becoming lodged in her throat. —Mrs Sarah Wilding, 58 Bentick- I street, said that deceased came to stay the day wita her on Friday last. She sent the child to fetch BOUM tread and whea she returned gave her a piece of bread and jam. That would be at about one o'clock. About 10 minntes after the child came hack crying, and apparently choking. She tried to put her finger down her throat,and patted her on the back, as witness noild see that the child was clinking. Subsequently she took the child lip in her arms with the inteation of taking her to Dr. Delany's, but was met by the Or. on the way.—Cacitsin James Heasley, Marine Superintendent, of the Manchester Ship Canal Conipahy said on Friday no was washing his hands when his daughter ran in and told him that " Louie Graham was dying" or "dead" ;he did not quite recollect the exact expression. He ran to the front gat, without stopping to wipe his hands, and saw the girl in the arms of some lady--(he believed her grandmoqier)—who was distrac- ted. anthexclaiming "She is dead !" "She is dead "! He took the child from her, and said • she was not dead. Then he rarawith her into his dining-room and with great difficulty opened her, mouth. Ile put his finger down but could find nothing. He also felt along the gullet but there was nothing there. Opening the child's mouth a second time he poured a little spirits do wnher throat, and that seemed to immediately restoie the heart's action, bat it Only beat, perhaps, for one, two,or even three feeble throbs.* Seeing then that the child was dead, he returned her to her grandmother and sent her to the li.ctor's. He afterwards heard that the Doctor •pronounced her dead.— Dr. J. B. Delaney, Runcorn, stated that h 3 went at once oa being summoned, and met Mrs Wilding. bringing the child, when he was by the South Bank Hotel. Toe child was then apparently dead. He got permission from the landlady of the South Bank Hots!, who ha would take the opportunity of saying was very kind in. deed, to take the child in there for the parpase of es- &mining it. Hs searched the back of the throat, and passed a probe into the stomach. Nothing c Line up, but he may have possibly Pushed something down. Meer- tattling that life was extinct he did nothing more. In his opinion, death was du s to asphyxia,—suffocating chok- ing, brought on by the child taking a sudden iaspiration —probably a fit of coughing coming on,and some portion of the bread became impacted in the larynx. This would I all °emir' in just as silent a space as he took to describe it and was sufficient to cattle suclea death.—The Coroner said that it was plainly a ease of " Accidental death" brought about in the manner described by the doctor.— Toe jury returned a verdict accordingly, and wished to have it recorded that they highly commended the con- duct of Captain Heasley, for his humans and prompt action in toe matter.

ANOTHER ACCIDENT ON THE GREAT ChNTRAL.

An alarming accident occurred on Wednesday at Victoria Station. Sneffield, on the Grea: Central Railway. A number of empty carriages were b3ing shunted on to the main line when, owing to a mistake the points were shifted, and a heavy bogey carriage ran off the line, ploughing up the permanent way and bursting the main gas supply, which Immediataly ignited. The down main lice was completely blocked for some hours. Yesterday morning the traffic was proceeding as usual

DEATH OF A CHESHIRE BARONET. Sir Charles Watkin Shakerley, Bart, C. 8., died at Sernerford Park, Cheshire, yesterday morning. Ile had been ailing for some time, but his death was not immediately expected. He was the second baronet, and married the daughter of Mrs. Ackers, of Moreton Hail, Cheshire. He leaves three sons, the eldest being Colonel Sttakerley, of Wilmslow. The deceased baronet ,was connected with the Fifth Cheshire Volunteers from the formation, and retired a few years ago with the rank of colonel. He was born in 1833, and succeeded Lis father, the first baronet, in 1857. In the year 1863 he filled the office of High Sheriff of Cheshire.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Wednet- day before Mr. It. T. Fairclough (in the chair) and Messrs. Be thought their wor- ships would not allow the ends of the law to be interfered with in that manner. Elizabeth Bowen said on the Saturday night in question she was going nut for sixpennyworth of brandy for her baby. She met a friend and went into a public houss. The plaintiff deserved to be punished but she was not charged. The defendant would have to go to gaol f.,r one month with hard labour.

THE BATTLE OF THE POLLS.

Sir,—We are now apperaching the Nevember Munich pal Elections, and the addresses of the reipective candi- dates on the hoardings are most amusing, if not instructive. Some of our past legislators speak prominently in the first person singular r—Cheep gas and other useful improvements which be, and he alone carried out, and finally passed into law for th 3 time being. Bath the Liberal and the Conservative vie with etch other in their desire and zeal to promote economy— th ! how elastic that word is—and rahe the moral stitch of the inhabi- tants, electors and non-electors. We are now being cajoled and flattered, but to my mind bamboozled by tbest promot r.Ft of cheap gas, &c. We have had these good and pious intmtions dinned into or ears in the past, but promises, like the proverbial pie crust, have been broken more than observed. One candidate dwells upon his carrying into effect the Park improvements, on Seethes as other days. Another dilates upon the fact that we now have three. pence per thousand feet redu:tion in gas. In a word such is the inventive genius of some of thesa municipal legislators that in future they purpose improving on the past by attending to labour matters, keeping the stan- dard rate of wales in force, and insisting on hiving the fair contract clause inserted in our by-laws, so that any and every job or contract must be given to the person who pays the standard rate of wages. To each of these resolves I say aye ! Again sir, some of our friends have disc irded their political colours (sea posters). Why ? Are they ashamed of a deep red—the emblem of their hope? Echo answers are they ? Now one word more and I have done in this connec- tion. In going threueh the names of our past and pre- sent representatives I fail to see one single name—or member of the large Catholic body of this town among our Town Councillors. Yet there are many members of that Church whose business ability and sterling worth are in every path of life unquestioned and un- questionable. That matters appertaining to the commu- nity in question will crop up from time to time, and with which their repr3sent3tive would alone be able to grasp intelligently is beyond the shadow of a doubt. Nevertheless no Catholic representative graces the Coun- cil Chamber. Elow is this ? hit for the want of thought. Again again, echo answers is it? Or perchance 'tie The good old rule, the simple plan That they should take who have the power And they should keep who can. Yours truly, J. P. K.

UPPER IIOWLEY-Tlll NIGHT SOIL COLLECTING VANS.

Sir,—Having recently complained of the nuisance the night soil collecting vans and of the nose the men created by shouting to one ancthsr, thus disturbing the neighbourhood, I think it only fair to the men to say that up to now there has been a markid improvement as far as they are concerned, and the vans go In4ck ward aid forward without us hearing a word from the men. If it were not for the noise of the wheels no one would known that the work was going on. I believe our thanks are due to the Chief Inspectors, who,when they bcame aware of it, determined to put it down at once. On behaf of the workingman in this neighbourhood, I sincerely thank them for the improvement so far. The rest I suppose must be left with the authorities.—Yours truly, S. DATIES.

===== 0002601_18981021_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WARRINGTON CORPORATION AND "THE FEATHERS" LICENSE.

Sir,—The decision of the Warrington Corporation at their last meeting, by 14, votes to 13, to carry out the praposed arrangement with a leading brewery firm, wil I am convincei, not meet with the apt proval of a large section of th 3 people of Warrington, when they know fully the details of such arrangement. The terms apparently are so much in favour of the firm in question, thst it is incom- prehensible to some, how a body or business men who form the Corporation of Warrington could for a moment even contemplate such an arrangement, let alme actu- ally enter into it. It is not at all surprising that when transactions of this nature are carried out there should be a painful impression created in the to vn that when the Corporation are dealing with certain firms, then the town's interests suffer materially. It is not for me to say whether such an impression be correct or not, but if the arrangement I refer to is carried out, then such an impression will certainly be strengthened. Although no doubt some of your readers are aware of the arrangement, yet there are many wto are not fully aware of all the details. So far as I was able to gather them at the meeting of the Councd, which I attended, they are as follows '• Messrs. Walker and Co. to give up a strip of land in Queen-street, and a strip of land in Bridge-street in ex- change for the Feathers license, and the transferring o the license to an intended new public-house in Orford- lane. Power, however, is reserved by Messrs. Walker and Co. of annulling the contract if the Bicenaine Bench do not consent to the transferring of the house to Orford-lane. The latter part of the arrangement would not have come to lieht if it had not been for the bold steed made by Councillor Tinnioe, who had literally to drag out th • information. RI was not at all surprising that certain members of the General Purposes Committee gave an onlooker the impression of being h.artily ashamed of the transaction—though they voted for it. It will be remembered that the monetary value of the Feathers license has generally been estimated at £3,000. The house and license have cest the town nearly £5,000. New what is the monetary value of the exchange? Well, Councillor Shaw Green,who ought to know,valued the Queen-se:act property which will have to be ouled down at £7oo,But it must be borne in mini that Messrs. Walks'. and Co. will be retaining a great portion of the lard on which the.3e houses steal. The Corporation only ootain the front strip. Then with reference to the strip of land in Bridge-street, this I understand is the mere removing of the railings in front of the Lion Rotel, and the addition to the footpath could net ha valued by any stretch of the imagination at £2OO. The town therefore. will not be obtaining in val re even £l,OOO in exchange. I am, therefore, borne to the con- clusion that if the proposed arrangement he carried out the town will suffer a loss of over £2,000. Coancillor Peacock places the figures at £5,000. And mind you there will be no extinguishing of a license in addition. And yet the General Purposes Committee worded their resolution "In the be.t interests of the town," Now I venture to ask the 14 members of the Corpora- tion who voted for such an arrangement, if there is one of them, if the Feathers Hotel had belonged to him would have entered into such an arrangement? I ven- ture to reply, and say no not one! The voting, I regret to say, bore the mark of being political: So long as men are weak enough to vote on party lines, the town's interests at times are bound to suffer and do suffer. In this case Councillir Bennett was an honourable exception,and the stand he made redounds much to his credit. I have not touched upon the higher morality of the question, althougt there is a great deal to be said on tnat side of it. I have simply taken up my pen to appeal to the 14 gentlemen to withdraw from their illogical position before it is tno late to save tho annals of the town from being disgraced. The arrangement from whatever light it may be looked at cannot be defended for one memsnt. As Alderman Henry Roberts from his words inferred "It would partake too much of the nature of a gift to the firm in question." Apf4logising for taking so much of your space,—l remain, yours faithfully, E. ENGLAND.

DROPPING} A LIGHTED MATCH ON A. BED. On Monday night the Warrington Fire Brigade were called to a fire at 16. Ship-yard, about ten minutes to six o'clock. They proceeded to the scene with a steam engine, and found that the flames, which were confined t) a bedroom, had been extinguished with buckets of wa at. It seems that the tenant of the house struck a match in the bed- room, and let it drop on the bedclothes, Petting them on fire. The Brigade returned as their services were not required. P.S.A. EARLESTOWN A.ND NEWTON.

SOCIAL EVENING.

A " Social " was held at the Mission Hall, Earles- town, oa Monday evening. There were about 40 persons present, and dancing was indulged in to the music supplied by Mr. J. H. Peplow. The evening was very enjoyable. EARLESTOWN _

The usual weekly meeting of the P.S.A., was held on Sunday, when there was a full attendance, Mr. W. Brown presided, and Mr. Taylor, Police Curt Mis.ionary (Warrington) was the principal speaker. Vocal items were given by Miss Woods,and recita- tions were given by Mrs. Andrews. Pomcn CASES. _

At the Earlestown Police Station on Monday morning before Mr. J. W. Emmett, Gerard Dowd, was committed for 14 days imprisonment for being drunk at Newton on Sunday, and assaulting Mr. W. Owen, landlord of the Millstone Inn. Thomas Joyce was fined 2s, 63., and costs for being drunk and disorderly at Newton on Saturday. WESLEYAN MISSIONARY SOCIETY. The annual missionary sermons were preached in the Wesleyan Chapel, Earlestown, on Sunday, by the Rev Pit Passmore. There were fair congrega- tions at each set rice, and at night the choir sang the anthem" How lovely are the Messengers." Collections were made in aid of the Wesleyan Missions. GOOD TEM:DIARY IT EABLESTOWN. The weekly meeting of the Hope of Earlestown Lodge of Good Templars, was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall, on Monday evening. Bra. R W Nichol- son presided. Four candidates were initiated to membership. Greetings wer3 received from the Dresden Victory Lodge, Staffordshire. Arrange- ments were made for the 26th anniversary tea and concert. Members were invited to attend the enter- tainment to be given at Ashton, by the Haydock lodge on Wednesday night. WELLINGTON 110 TEL PICNIC CLUB. A smoking concert in connection with the above club took place at the Wellington Hotel, Earles- town, on Thursday evening. Mr. Anders presided, and the following programme was submitted : Overture, Mr. H. Curran; song, "Monarch of the Woods," Mr. W. Rigby; song." Remember me," Mr. E. H. Healer; song, " Queen cf the earth," Mr. R. Franklin; song," The Skipper and his boy," Mr. W. Anders; dnet."lmitation ot bagpipes," Mr.C.Dicken- son and Mr. H. Curran; song, "The joys of gold,"Mr. J. Vandry; selection on the bones, Mr.C. Dickenson; song, "The white squall," Mr.W.Rigby; song, "Some day I'll visit old Ireland," Mr.P.Lynch; song, "When she goes to Bee Bo," Mr. J. Vandry; sang, "The Diver," Mr. R. Franklin ; duet, " Alice, where art thou ?" Messrs. Dickenson and Curran; song," The song that reached my heart," Mr. W.Rigby. SMOKING CONCERT AT HAIRLBSTOWN. The first of the winter series of smoking con- certs promoted by the Earlestown Conservative Club took place on Friday evening in the large billiard room of the club. Mr. W. Collingvrood pre- sided and there was a large attendance of members. The whole of the items on a lengthy programme ' were excellently submitted. THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, OCTOBER 21 1898.

FATALITY AT EA.RLESTOWN.

A FATAL FALL.

On Saturday morning a man named William Water- man, 60 years of age, employed as a carpeater at the Viadue; Works, and who resided at 3, Chandos-street, Earlestown, was engaged repairing a wagon, when he fell through the Wagon frame, his head coming in con- tact with one of the wheels. When attended to Ise appeared to be unconscious, but revived, and he was assisted up. As be appeared to heirs a serious condition he was placed oa the ambulance litter, and taxers hems. Medical assistance was sent for, but before Dr. Ei andy- side arrived, the unfortunate man had expired. The inquest was held at the Wellington flo:el, E srle s- town on Tusclay morning by Mr. S. Brighouse. Mr. Tinker, HM, Inspector of Factories was present, and Mr. Bland watched the inq riry on behalf of the L. and N.W. Railway Company. The Coroner opened the enquiry with a brief statement of facts, whdrti were a, above. He said he thought be should like to have a look at the wagon himself and did so. The wagon was a skeleton,and be drew a rough skstch for the information of the jury, showing, the p esition of the wagon fr and explained how it was supposed the man had fallen. It was surmised, he said, that he was kneeling on the longitudinal, and his hand was resting on the diagonal, and it was supposed that the latter had dropped down. and the man had fallen forward striking his bead against the wheel, which was only 10 inches below the frame. _ _ _

Mn. Mary An Waterman,widow of the deceased,said ha resided at 3, Chandos-street, Eartestown. His age was 59 vears,and he was a wagon mill?r for the London and North-Western Railway Company. H 3 had worked for the Company upwards of 20 years. He left holllP at five minutes to nine on Saturday morning, and was in good health, and ate a very hearty breakfast. He was brought home dead shortly before tea o'clock. He was not troubled with any kind of seizure or giddiness. John Barlow rail he and deceased were workin.g at a coal wagon, and were getting a diagonal out. The dia- gonal was broken at one end, and they had to get a lot of bolts out,after which they made preparations for get- ting the headstock off. Ha (witness) went to get the spanner,and Waterman went to the saw to get a template cut for the diagonal. He saw Waterman come back and get in the wagon, and son after witness heard a crash, and saw deceased on ths door, and found that the dia- gonal had broken and Waterman had fallsn throudh. He asked deceased if he was lion, but he never spoke, and died before leaving the works. A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned.

THE APPROACHING VISIT OF THE RH.ONDDA. ROYAL GLEE PARTY.

A musical treat is in store for those who attend Parr Hall on Thursday evening next when the Rhondda Royal Glee Society will provide a grand evening concert. The choir is admittedly the finest combinatioa of Welsi vocalists which have aver , been drawn together. It is the only male voice society which has won four national Eisteddfodau first prizes including the 1.000 dollars and gold medal at the World's Fair, Chicago. It has bad the distinction of singing before Her Majesty the Queen at Windsor Castle.Slost of the performers have been twice to America,and have frequently toured in the Unite,' Kingdom. 51r.Toin Stspheas, the conductor, when questioned by Her Majesty on the occasion of the visit anti led to said that three-fourths of the singers were miners. Her Majesty was delightsd wiAl the singing and asked fora repetition of " The Men of Elarlech." As her Majesty was retir- ing she handel to her German librarian, Mr.Muther, a small case containing a beautiful scarf pin for Mr. Tom Stephens as a memento of the occasion. It is a gold pin, mounted with diamonds and rubies, forming the royal crown, surmounting the initials " V.R.1." A large solid silver case for Mr. W. A. Morgan followed. Mr. Stephens vvas also asked to follow the Queen into an ante room and write his name in her birthday book. Noticing that he was a little flurriel her Majesty bade him take plenty of time,and said there were plenty of pnis to choose from. In coming out of the state apartments into St. George's Hall, Mr. Stephens was button- holed by Sir Walter Parratt, the Queen's organist, and enthusiastically congratulated upon the great impression which the concert had created. "Of all the choirs I have seen," declared Sir Walter, " I have never yet seen one so completely under the command of the conductor as yours have been to night." " I told you," continuad the Queen's or- ganist, turning to a gentleman wao stool by him. "the people ot Wales can sing even English music much better than the English people themselves. This has been one of the greatest musical treats of ,my life," and as he said 93 he grasped the hands of the happy little Welshman and cordially shook , them. And do you think," chimed in Mr. Muther " that Windsor is too far for you to visit us again." "Why." responded Mr. Tom Stephens with alacrity "we shall only be too delighted." The choir will be assisted at Warrington. by the following first-class artistes :—Madame Williams-Penn, It A.M., Mr. DaviA Jones, A..R.A.31., Mr. Tern Salsthiel, and Eas Dar (the renowned singer of ancient Welsh verse. Violin solos will be contributed by Hiss %label 'ransley, of the Royal conservatorium of Music. Leipzig, and Miss Bessie Jones will perform on the harp An excellent pro- gramme has been arranged, and there is every promise of a most enjoyable raveling's entertain- ment. The proceeds are in aid of the Y.M.O A.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MR. CHARLES TWISS.

The death of Mr. Charles Twiss, the headmaster of People's College Schools, Warrington, removes from our midst one of the best-known men in the town. His death was startling in its suddenness. While making preparations at the school last Satur- day morning for a pupil teachers' examination Mr. Twiss was seized suddenly with illness. He was at once conveyed to his home in Cairo-street, and Drs. White and Fox were summoned. Despite the most skilful nursing and medical attention, his condition grew rapidly worse,and he succumbed to his illness at six o'clock on Sunday evening, death being due to acute congestion of the lungs and syncope. He was 59 years of age. As a mark of respect the flags at the Town Hall and Liberal Club were hoist-d half mast. In our last is ,us we reportel a speech by Mr.Twies made at the founiation stone laying of new branch schools at. Bank (bay. He was then looking in the best of ' health and spirits, and made a characteristically , happy speech. Now he is no more ! Within one short week his friends have gathered round his grave and his remeins have found their last resting place. The deceased gentleman was of a most retiring disposition. He disliked anything that savoured of prominence or publicity,and took no active part in questions of a political or religious nature. He rarely spoke in public, and then only on matters pertaining to educational work—Oß which his opinions were most valuable. But _nevertheless he was a popular man. He was known and respected throughout the town and district. His popularity was almost entirely due te the manner in which he discharged the duties of headmaster of People's College. Mr. Tense was a born schoolmas- ter. Never hal a school a more conscientious or devoted servant than he. His guiding principles were diligence, perseverance and indefatigability. 11,s whole energy was given freely t everything which c mcerne I the welfare of his schoel. Regu- larity and punctuality were distinguishing features of his life, and their importance to the scholars he inculcated by his own example. Such a man de- Served success and Mr. Twiss' s schools were success- ful in a remarkable degree. The development of People's College from very humble beginnings has been largely due to Mr. Twiss, and the high standard of excellence which it has attained has done much to redeem Warrington as an educa- tional centre from an unenviable position. Mr. Twiss was a Warrington man. He commenced his education at a school then existing in Newton- street, the head master of which at that time was tne late Mr. Edwara Brewtnall. Mr. Twiss became a pupil teacher at the same school, and after com- pleting his apprenticeship he went to the Borough. road Training College, Lend on. 0 i the completion of his college career he took charge of schools at St. Helens and Bowdon. But upon the death of Mr. Haydn he was appointed headmaster of the People's College, at the close of the year 1870. The old Newton-street School had then hem pulled down to afford a site for the present Central Station and until the erection of a portion of the buildings which to-day constitute the People's College the scholars were taught in the Buttermarket-street Wesleyan School and in the Golborne-street school. About 1871 the school in Arpley-street was ready for occupation, and at the commencement there were little more than 200 scholars on the books. As illus- trating the development of the school during Mr. T wise's time as head master it may be stated that the number of scholars to-day is over 1,000, The school itself has during that time undergone great altera- tions. In the first place the room leading from the front porch was used as an infants' school, but as time went on and the number of scholars rapidly increased infants' schools were established in Cairo- street and at Bank Quay to relieve the parent school. Scholars were then only taken from the first standard at People's College. It was subse- gently found necessary to add two large rooms to the exi-ting buildings, and at a more recent date a large room for cookery lessons. Under Mr. Twiss's careful supervision the school has always had most gratifying reports from her Majesty's inspectors, and on several occasions special mention has been made of it in Blue Books. Alr.Twiss took an active interest in everything which tended to the intel- lectual improvement of young and old. On two occasions he was invited by the Cemmission ap- pointed by the Government to consider questions of over-pressure in day-schools and the working of the pupil teacher system, to give evidence, and he complied te both invitations Throughout the 28 years of his headmastership of People's College, Mr. Twiss scrupulously upheld the unFectarian principles of the school which have been a most prominent characteristic of its career, and have added not a little to its success. In his numerous scholars Mr. Twiss took a great interest. Nor did it end when they had passed through the school. He watched their careers and encouraged them by a kindly ward. Mr. TWit33 was repeatedly asked by employers of labour and professional men to suggest suitable lads for various posts, and he was generally able to put his hands on the right one. He was always delighted to hear of the success of an old scholar, and it is not too much to say that they are now to be found scattered in all parts of the globe. No scholar ever left his school who did not cherish and carry through life with him a deep affection and respect for his old head-master. lie might have had unpleasant experiences of his master's strictnees of discipline, but these were forgotten in the con- sciousness of the lasting good he had received, and of a debt which he could never repay. The following letter which Mr. Twist] wrote last Friday is the last he ever penned and is typical of his generosity and thoughtfulness. It was addressed to Councillor A. Woods as chairman of the Art Ex- hibition Committee, and was as follows: " People's k College, Warrington, October 14th, 1898,—Dear Sir. I shall have much pleasure in sending the scholars to the exhibition of pictures on Friday afternoon next, the 22nd inst., and beg to enclose cheque for the amount. f think the committee have taken a very desirable step in arranging for the children of the various schools to visit the exhibition at so reasonable a subscription.-1 am, yours faithfully, Cr/ARLES Twiss.” _ TUE FUNERAL.

REFERENCE AT THE LITERARY AND • PHILOSOPHICAL SOCIETY'S MEUING.

Kindly reference was made to the death of Mr. Charles Tiviss at the opening meeting c f the Warrington Liter- ary and Philosophical Society on Monday evening. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. R. Young. The CHAIRMAN said they had once more to record the loss of a very valued membsr of the society. During the two years he (Mr. Young) was president, too fre- quently was it his duty to mike known the loss they were sustaining by the death of valued members ef their society. Probably they would all be aware that Mr. Charles Twiss,head master of the People's College,passed away on the previous day. Mr. Twiss had b3en a mem- ber of thet soctety something like 15 or 16 years, but he had never succeeded in inducing him t 3 offer them a piper, and those who keew him well knew why. A more sensitive and retiriag man he never met, and he perfectly abhorred anything that Favoured of promin- ence or publicity. If evidence, however, were needed of Mr. Twiss's ability it was seen in the school they knew as the People's College, in the illitnitude of his , scholars and in the splendour of his annual reports. Those who knew him intimately, as he was privileged to do, understood how absolutely he sacrificed himself for the echoel. He (Mr. Young) happened to be a mem- bsr of the committee of the school, and he was in a positioa to say that on all occasions the interests of the school and his scholars occupied the first position in his mind. He was also in a position to say that more than once or twice the committee had to almost compel Mr. Twiss to think somewhat of his own pardeular interests. They would agree with him that tht occasion should not be passed over without expressing their sympathy with the bereaved family of a very honoured member. The Rev. MORLEY STEvENsom, president of the society, said he would like to add a word or two to what their ex-President had said with regard to the late Mr. Twiss. Lie heartily re-echoed every word that had fatlen from him. He (Mr. Sievens in) happened to have been awe clat el with Mr Twisi in matters of common educational work for &considerable time, and he would like to say that he did not think he had ever in his life met anyon& who was so censcientious in the parformance of his duties, who so succeeded in giving himself up to the work which he had taken in hand, and who set such an example of a whole devotion to duty as did Mr. Twiss. He must have earned the respect of all who came in con- tact with him,not only in the profession he adorned, but as a good citizen awl a good man.Thcseiwere not exagger- ated words. He meant frem his heart the simple words he had uttered. Not only had that society sustained a less, but the town had sustained a loss also, which they would find out more and more as time went on.

LINES ON THE DEATH OF CHARLES TWISS.

Hear ye that knell,the deep-ton'd cry of death? Aglow and mournful dirge,with note profound; List, as it tolls its ever solemn sound, It ushers an immortal one to earth! Stay gentle muse, inspire the sacred line Of praise to him,whose name is on the quiv'ring lip Of many here who knew him—a sad scene, So sudden, so severe his loss! Who was A modest mam,—a novelty—an honest man, More rare ; and as we mourn his loss, we say We do not bury Rood men everyday. For they are few, and tears that in the eye Now sparkle, prove how he was loved, By old and young, a learned friend and true, A citizen of unsullied life, In good repute, and pleasant with all men. Say he was gentle, kind, agreeable, We may not life recall, but truth to say, That even his failings leaned to virtue's side ! Stockton Heath.

--,-- TOUE OF BARNUM AND BAILEY'S SIIOW.

We have received a well-printed folding— Mi-tp of England and Scotland, on which red lines indicats the route taken by " the greatest show on earth" during the present tour through the United King- dom. The tour will close at Stoke on November 12, and it appear.; that 530 performances have beenl given, while the weather interfered with only ten performances. The number of miles travelled will he 2,976, the longest run being 208 and the shortest five miles,while sixteen railways have been utilised. The company consists of 840 persons, there are 420 horses and ponies, 25 cages of wild animals, 20 elephants and 18 camels. The tents number 21, the circus tent accommodating 14,864 people. The total number of waggons with the show is 103, and the vast organisation moves about by means of 67 cars, each 54 feet in length.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

CAIRO STREET CHAPEL.

On Sunday evening, in the Cairo-street Chapel, the Rev. W. H. Dummond preached the third of a course of sermons on Christianity and Democracy," taking for his subject "Democractic duties." The Rev. gentleman said the first thry which should lie upon the heart of every man who had awakened to the privileges of his own life and the dangers of the lives of his fellows was alertness of mind and spirit—more watchfulness. How many were the dangers, difficalties, distress, and corruptions, simply because na.4l had gone to sleep and refused to take the trouble to take an intelligent interest in the great social liie which was going on around them. Thesecond duty was to emphasize the place of character in public life, and to the traming of cisaracter in them- selves.

BOLD STREET' WE3LEYAN CHURCEL

On Sunday, (children's Sunday) at this church, the Rev. H. G. Robercs, conducted a teacher's prayer meet- ing at 7 a.m. Preaching at the ordinary morning service to a good congregation the rev. gentleman said the recognised duty of Christian society towards children was one result of the beneficent forces of Christianity. It was only when they contrasted the happy child life of this country with that of the heathen world that they could realise what chillho -id owed to the teaching and example of Christ. There was little thought of the sacredness of child-life before the time of our Lord. Referring to Rome udder Augustus (one of the I golden ages of the world), the preacher showed , that there was general disregard if the claims of children. The family was God's type of every com- munity. Naturally, at:faction was the organising principal ot the family in those days. God had invested them with great responsibilities. One of the greatest I missions in the world was to teach the child, of God and truth. There was no diviner work in the world. Re- ferring to Napoleon, Hume, Voltaire and Augustine, the preacher demonstrated that the evening of life was to a large extent what the morning had made it. The hope of the world was centred on the child. Round the cradle of Bethlehem gathered the hopes of the world In closing the preacher made an appeal to the young people to make a decisive choice. Purpose was the eternal condition of success. It was a sad thing to begin life with a low conception of it. I There was no tragedy like that of a wasted life.—The preacher conducted a servic e in the schoolroom in the 1 aftvraoon. Ic was very impressive and much good was , done. Great hope was entertained by the preacher con- cerning the children.—ln the evening he to -1 his text from Luke 2 49-52 and presented Christ as an example to young people. The preacher referred in sdathing terms to those young people who had no ambition, and sought pleasure in parading what he called the Sabbatie promenade of Warrington (Sankey-street). Mr. Levi Richardson sang "Come unto me." with considerable ex- pression. The services of the day were most successful and caluulared to inspire the young people —with whom the Rev. H. G. Roberts particularly desires to get into closer touch—to a hicbe• and nobler life.

CONCERT.

The eoncert season was openel in Wartington on Saturday hot, on the occasion of the annual concert held at Parr Hall in aid of the Warrington Infirmary and Dul- -1 pensary. Ford seconded, "That the best thanks of this Lodge be tendered to those who have taken pan," after which the meet- ing was closed by singing and prayer. WARRINGTON PHILATELIC FOCIETY.

The third annual general meeting of this society was held on Monday evening at the Patten Arms Hotel. The President (Mr. John P. Reynolds) took the chair, and there was a fair attendance of mem- bers. Mr. Herbert Woods, the hon. secretary, pre- sented a very satisfactory report of the past year's work ; the balance sheet also showed that the finances of the society are on a sound basis. The membership continuos to increase but the secretary is always glad to hear of new members. The retir- ing officers were re-elected. Arrangements pave been made to hold periodical meetings during the winter months when pipers will be read and matters of philatelic interest discussed. Tni PROPAGATIoN OF SPIRITUALISM.

The Spiritualist Society have instituted a lyceum for the training of children in what they believe to be the principles of truth. On Sunday morninga i commencement was made, and they succeeded n making the meeting interesting to those present. In the afternoon there were gool audiences to listen to Mr. Ben Plant, of Manchester,and in the evening especially delivered a gool address, his subject being " Why do spirits return ?" Alter each ad- dress he gave a number of clairvoyant delineations. On Monday evening the hall was crowded. After the usual opening exercises Mrs. Byles gave an in- teresting account of how Sil,3 became a Spiritualist, after which she gave several clairvoyant descriptions of departed friends and a couple of successful psy- chometric experiments. " THE SECOND COILING OF OUR LORD." The third of a eirie3 of public lectures on this subject was delivered on Monday evening in the Assembly Room at the Conservative Club by Mr. Hall of Birkenhead. He said that on the previous Monday he spoke to them on the approaching judg- ment and the great apostacy in all society which was provoking that judgment. They had for their subject that night the way of escape from that im- pending judgment. If they looked back on the past history of.mankiud they would notice that always before a time of judgment God had done a work of mercy. He had seat a full warning and prepared a way of escape. Looking at their condition he thought such was needed at the present time. Where was the clitireh of the living God which he sent forth in the beginning? It was rent and torn into a hundred sects; its glory had departed and others besides the Lord exercised authority over it. It would he singular if at such a time God did not do something for his scattered and divided ministry.

Be had served tne rown Hall. Park, and Baths Committee with greater pleasure than any other committee of the Corporation. He was made a member when he was first elected to the Council, 17 years ago. He was proposed by Mr. John Pierpoint, and had been on the committee ever since. The duties had sometimes been very pleasant, and at other times full of trouble. They would remember that the scheme for pulling down the wall in front of the Town Hell gave the Com- mittee a great deal of trouble. But he was pleased when the work was accomplished. The committee had always carried out their work as well as noel possibly could. So far as he was concerned, they would have the benefit of his experience in garden' lug and other matters as long as he remained on the Council. (Hear, bear.) BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. mBNICIPAL ELECTION, NOVEMBER lsr, 1898. T" ELECTORS OF THE TOWN HALL WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN, Baying been requested by a large number of the ratepayers of the ward to offer myself as a candidate for the vacancy at the ensuing Municipal Election, after due con- sideratiOn, I have decided to do so. Being a resident and ratepayer In the ward, I claim to know its wants, and if elected, its interests shall at all times have my best attention BOROUGH IMPROVEMENTS. I believe that by careful and good management. efficiency with economy can be obtained, and that the Rates of the Borough. Whieh are now too high, can be reduced without interfering with necessary improvements, and also that it is desirable that some portion of to projected street Improv e- meats should be forthwith completed. INTRODUCTION OF TRAMWAYS. The laying down of Tramways in the Borough is an urgent necessity, and, In my opinion, could be made in a short time a profitable source of revenue, a benefit to tradespeople, and a boon to the public. THE GAS QUESTION. The Council may not necessarily be weakened by the introduction of younger members, and the reduction In the prim of gas which comes into operation on November let was undoubtedly brought about by the energetic and persistent action of the young men in the Council. If elected, it shall be my earnest endeavour, not only to obtain an improvement in the quality, but also a further reduction in the price. My action upon the Council, if you do me the honour to elect me as your representative shall be unbiassed by party considerations, and influenced only by a desire to be of some service to my fellow townsmen. I am, ladles and gentlemen, Your obedient Servant. JAMES HORTON. Crosfield-street, Warrington, October 14th, 1898.

MUNICIPAL RLIWTION. 1898. TO THE ELECTORS OF BEWSEY WARD. LADIES AND GESTLTMEN, Haring been requested by a large number of representative Ratepayers and workingmen to offer myself as a Candidate at the coming Election of a Councillor for your Ward. I have much pleasure in accepting the invitation ; and as I have lived in the Ward for the past 29 years, I may claim to know something of its requirements. If you de me the honour to elect me I shall endeavour to secure cleaner and cheaper gas, better sanitary arrangeraents, also a continuance of tile improvements in paving and light- ing back passages which were commenced sic years ago and all matters affecting the interest of the Ward and Town generally shall receive my earnest consideration. Appealing with confidence for your support on the Ist November neat I am, ladles and gentlemen Yours respectfully. T. B. MALKIN. ! 95, Demo:l-road, Warrington.

MUNICIPAL ELECTION, NOVEMBER. 1898. TO THE ELECTORS OF LATCHFORD WARD. LADIES IND GENTLEMEN, I beg to thank you for the honour conferred upon me in electing me as your representative in our Town Council for the past three years. Luring my term of office I have, to the best of ray ability. carded out your instructions. My devotion to your service will be observed by attention to the attested statement of attendanees at Council and Committee Meetings for 1895-6, 1895-7, 1897-8. 1895-6. 1896-7. No. of No. of No. of No. of Atten. Meet.. Atten. Meet. Council Meetings 13 13 Council Meetings 19 19 School Attendance 18 20 School Attendance 19 20 Museum, to 11 14 Museum, Ac 11 16 Town Hall, fia 22 25 Town Hall, fig 20 23 64 72 69 78 1897-8 up to and including 22nd September, 1893. No. of No. of Atten. Meet. Council Mietings 14 14 School Attendance 18 18 Museum, ,to 10 12 Town Hall, ,ko 21 21 -- 63 65 Total Meetings 215. Total Attendances 198. I have also promoted the formation and paving of our new streets and back passages, and the erection of additional gas lamps. The Council also adopted a motion proposed by myself that a recreation ground be provided for our Ward, which led up to the purchase of the Old Warps Estate. This has been dedicated to, and a portion set apart far the use of the people. and called Victoria Park. All these improve- ments will add to the health, happiness, and individual contentment of the people, which should be the object of all Local Government. Much yet remains to be accomplished, and on the First of November you will be called upon to elect your representative for this important Ward. A large number of my friends have requested an 3 to allow myself to be nominated for re-election, which I have consen- ted to do. Bkoald you again honour ma with your confidence I shall, to the best of my ability, do all I can to promote the best interests of the elector.. It Is a source of gratification to me to males the progress which has been made in our Ward duriag the sixty years of my residence amongst you, and I confidently appeal to you for your vote and Interest to again place main the honourable position of your represen- tative. I beg to remain, Ladies and Gentlemen, your obedient servant, LUKE E LLISON, 639, Knutsford-road, Latchford.

MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS, 1898. TO THE BURGESSES OF ST. JOHN'S WARD. LADIES Inn GENTLEMEN.— At the desire of your Ward Committee and with the unanimous approval and support of the Conservatives of the Ward. I have much pleasure in °daring you my services as a representative of the Ward. It will be my endeavour at all times to see that the sanitary condition of the ward, includ- ing the paving of streets and back passages and lighting of sarae, obtains prompt attention. I am in favour of good wages and fair prices, and I condemn sweating in every shape and form. I shall ke ever ready to receive suggestions from any of the burgesses of the ward, and where any grievance er object detrimental to the true interests ef the burgesses exists, I shall give UM] most strenuous opposition until It be removed. Should you do me the honour to cleat me, my constant aim in the Town Connell will be to do all in my power for the improvement of the town and its trade, so as to create labour aud wages for the working classes, and business for the shopkeepers and commercial interest. I therefore with Confidence appeal t) you for your votes and support on the day of election. I am, Ladles and Gentlemen, Tours faithfully, JOHN BAIRD. Laburnum-villa. Pros/hall-lane, Warrington, October 12th 1898.
